Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?
2026-09-21

Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng of Ohio State University discuss whether an internship or a degree is the correct choice for career growth. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) College students are facing one of the most precarious labour markets in years. A recent New York Federal Reserve study pointed to a sharp increase in the post-college unemployed compared with other workers, with explanations ranging from artificial intelligence job displacement to a broader slowdown in hiring. One response has been a boom in trade-school and apprenticeship programmes that launch young people into ‘AI-proof’ careers. But students at community and four-year colleges can also beat these headwinds and launch a career through on-the-job internships. We’re a group of multidisciplinary scholars at The Ohio State University who study two paid internship programmes in science, technology, engineering and math, or STEM, with a proven record of landing jobs for students. Although the two initiatives use different models, they both illustrate four lessons that colleges and employers can use to turn internships into genuine career pathways. Just as notable is that a large share of these students come from diverse and underrepresented backgrounds, with many being the first in their families to go to college. These programmes can serve as a powerful equaliser for young people who may not otherwise have such career opportunities. Internships remain one of higher education’s most powerful tools for preparing students for work. Decades of research show that students who complete high-quality internships are more likely to secure job interviews and high-earning positions post-graduation. At the same time, the number of internship opportunities is diminishing, even though that preparation matters more than ever. Artificial intelligence is transforming entry-level work, leading employers to increasingly value the human skills that machines cannot easily replicate: judgement, adaptability, collaboration and the ability to solve unfamiliar problems. Academic leaders are recognising these pressures. More than one-third of university presidents and two-thirds of community college presidents told an Inside Higher Ed survey that they plan to add or expand apprenticeship pathways over the next three years. But it’s not just the number of internships that matters – internship quality counts just as much. Our research at the College Impact Laboratory examined learning through experience with two multi-institutional studies. One is Bridging Academic Training Through Experiential Research and Innovation, or BATTERI, led by principal investigator Jay Sayre and project manager Joanna Gardner. The other is Drive to Thrive. Across both initiatives, students benefited when their experiences combined substantive work with mentoring and professional development, rather than being treated as inexpensive labour. This distinction suggests that merely placing students in workplaces isn’t enough. The structure and quality of those experiences help determine whether internships lead to meaningful, high-earning careers. Drive to Thrive deploys an approach called ‘earn-as-you-learn’. Through partnerships with Ford dealerships, students alternate between classroom instruction and paid workplace experience. They work alongside experienced technicians, diagnose and repair customers’ vehicles and receive regular coaching. In this sense, it’s similar to the European model of traineeships for young people. This initiative reached 635 students at 18 community colleges, including 345 first-generation students and 300 students of colour. Through interviews and focus groups, a majority told us the internship helped them see themselves as true professionals. And several said the internship confirmed they had chosen the right career. The numbers also backed up our approach. Students in the earn-as-you-learn programme were almost 50pc more likely to graduate compared with students in a matched comparison group. Our other programme brings together clean energy manufacturing students from three different types of institutions – a major research university, a historically Black university and a community college – for a 10-week internship. The programme combines workplace learning with professional development workshops, peer networking, community engagement and projects with industry partners such as Honda, Stellantis and Schaeffler. This initiative took a distinctive approach by assigning multiple mentors to each student. The idea is to help students develop relationships with faculty members, technical experts, employers and classmates. This network, in turn, strengthened students’ confidence, professional identity and sense of belonging while complementing the technical skills they develop. To date, the formula has worked. More than half the participants in 2025 have already lined up either a new internship or job interviews. Industry partners also plan to rehire several members of the 2026 cohort as interns, extending their training beyond our programme and opening pathways into clean-tech careers. Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Based on this record, we believe that colleges should build broader support networks around internships rather than expect a single supervisor to shoulder the entire responsibility for a student’s professional development. In both programmes, four valuable principles emerged. First is the importance of meaningful work. We believe interns should tackle real organisational challenges with deliverables that matter beyond the classroom. For example, two interns spent the summer addressing a safety challenge for Honda – how to prevent battery cells from catching fire and cooling them down when they do. After developing potential solutions, they presented their work to industry representatives. A senior engineer from the Honda Research Institute was so impressed that he contacted them about a post-internship opportunity. That sense of consequence changed how the students approached the work. Rather than viewing it as an assignment to complete, they were making a contribution that others depended on. Second is the constellation of support for students. The liaison between the institution and industry partners is essential, serving as the connective tissue that holds the internship together and ensuring that students don’t fall through the cracks. But that liaison alone isn’t enough. Students also need access to faculty, industry supervisors, technical experts and peers, since each brings a different kind of guidance. One strength of the clean energy programme, for example, is its inclusion of industry experts across institutions and organisations. Students benefit most when they can draw on multiple mentors and peer support to develop key collaborative skills. Third, students should learn how to normalise struggle. Internships aren’t a test of whether students can already function as professionals. They’re environments for students to try, fail and retry. When educators emphasise struggle as part of the learning process, students are encouraged to avoid perfectionism and instead ask for help when difficulties arise. For example, students submit weekly reports during the clean energy programme that give them the chance to discuss challenges, troubleshoot problems together and recognise that uncertainty is part of becoming a professional. Finally, it’s vital to make interns’ contributions visible. Presentations, project showcases and recognition from industry professionals reinforce that students’ work matters while strengthening their professional identities. We believe universities and industries alike should heed these lessons, which extend well beyond clean energy manufacturing and automotive technology. Successful programs build ecosystems that often determine whether students simply complete an internship or launch a career. As colleges respond to changing labour markets, they should resist measuring success just by how many internships they create. Instead, they should ask whether those opportunities help students become confident, capable professionals. Internship quantity opens the door to better outcomes after college. Internship quality changes lives. By Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng Prof Matthew J Mayhew is a professor of higher education at The Ohio State University. He is interested in how collegiate conditions, educational practices and student experiences influence learning and democratic outcomes, including: moral reasoning; pluralism; productive exchange across worldview differences; and innovation capacity. To support the study of college and its impact on student development and learning, Mayhew has been awarded more than $16m in funding from sources including but not limited to the US Department of Education, the Ewing Marion Kauffman Foundation, the Andrew C Mellon Foundation, the Fetzer Institute and the Merrifield Family Foundation. Dr Courtney DeRoche is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University’s College Impact Laboratory, where she leads the ‘Drive to Thrive: Student Success in Career and Technical STEM’ project. Yun-Han Weng is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University. Her research focuses on collegiate environments, STEM education and internationalisation. She has been in the field for 10 years and has studied how different interventions and different collegiate climates impact students’ sense of belonging and learning outcomes. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Related: The Conversation, working life, Opinion, skills, higher education, Guest Column, internships, education, research

Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations
2026-09-21

Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Users could have been unaware that their data was being used for ads, said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. Ireland’s data protection watchdog has fined Google €403m and ordered the tech giant to become compliant with the region’s privacy regulations within six months. The Data Protection Commission (DPC), concluding a six-year-long probe, found that Google did not comply with the EU’s GDPR when processing data generated in the bloc. Its inquiry, triggered by complaints from European consumer rights organisations, including from BEUC, probed how the company processed location data across its ‘web & app activity’, ‘location history’ and ‘location accuracy’ features between May 2018 and February 2020. ‘Web & app activity’ is a Google account setting that processes information related to users’ activity on Google services, including sites and apps, meanwhile ‘location history’ tracks users’ locations and processes the data as visited places, activities and paths between locations. The complaints raised concerns relating to the “legality of Google’s processing of location data and the transparency surrounding that processing”, the DPC said in a statement announcing the probe in 2020. The probe found that Google infringed the GDPR by failing to lawfully process its location data, failing to demonstrate its compliance with the GDPR and failing to meet its transparency obligations across the features in question. “Location data can bring both benefits and harms to individuals,” said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. “It can greatly enhance the utility of online services, but it can also reveal a significant amount of information about an individual, including information that is inherently private. “The GDPR provides a high level of protection of personal data throughout the EEA, and requires that the processing of personal data must be carried out in a lawful, fair and transparent manner. “As a result of Google’s failures in this regard, individuals could have been unaware that their location was being used to, for example, influence them with ads or to infer their interests, and could lose control over their personal data. The retention of users’ location data for longer than necessary aggravated this loss of control.” In a statement to SiliconRepublic.com, a Google spokesperson said that the case centres around “historical policies” that have been updated since. “From 2019 onwards, we’ve significantly evolved our practices and launched robust tools that make managing location data simple,” the company said. In 2019, Google introduced auto-delete controls that allow users to automatically delete data on a rolling three-, 18- or 36-month basis. In 2023, it updated its location history feature to enable timelines to be stored directly on users’ devices. Google also said it does not store precise location data in ‘web & app activity’ and allows users to manage how their data is used for ads. This is the DPC’s fourth largest fine since the GDPR came into effect. In 2023, Meta received the largest fine issued by the watchdog to date, raking in €1.2bn in penalties after illegally transferring Facebook user data from the EU to the US. Last year, TikTok was fined €530m for transferring personal user data to China, while Meta was also subject to a separate €405m fine in 2022 for publicly disclosing contact details of children using Instagram’s business accounts. UK AI infrastructure start-up Nscale files for US IPO

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Nscale’s data centre in Glomfjord, Norway. Image: Nscale The Financial Times reported that the company is seeking ‘a valuation of up to $35bn’. UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale has filed for a US initial public offering (IPO) through a statement of registration to the country’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC). The number of shares to be offered and their price range have not yet been determined, according to the company, which has applied to list its ordinary shares on the New York Stock Exchange under the ticker symbol ‘NSCL’. The Financial Times, however, reported on Friday (18 September) that Nscale is seeking “a valuation of up to $35bn” in the IPO, citing people familiar with the matter. Goldman Sachs, JP Morgan and Morgan Stanley will act as lead bookrunners for the proposed offering. RBC Capital Markets, Deutsche Bank Securities and TD Securities, among others, will act as other bookrunners, while Citizens Capital Markets, Loop Capital Markets and Roth Capital Partners, among others, will act as the offering’s co-managers, Nscale said. According to its SEC filing, the London-based AI start-up had a net loss of $1.02bn on revenue of $140.6m for the six months ending on 30 June; for the same period in 2025, it had a net loss of $368.9m on revenue of $10.4m. “As of 31 August 2026, our infrastructure portfolio included approximately 25,000 active GPUs and 461,000 active and contracted GPUs, five active and 12 contracted data centre sites (including seven wholly-owned sites, nine co-location sites and one leased site) and approximately 1.37GW of active and contracted capacity (representing 1GW at owned sites, 200MW at leased sites and 165MW at co-location sites),” Nscale’s filing read. It added that the company’s footprint “is concentrated in renewable-rich, low-cost power regions such as Norway, Portugal, Iceland and select locations in the United States and APAC”. The company raised $2bn in Series C funding at a valuation of $14.6bn in March of this year. At the time, it said the money would be used to accelerate its global development of vertically integrated AI infrastructure – including GPU compute, networking, data services and orchestration software – across Europe, North America and Asia. Nscale has AI infrastructure deployment deals with Microsoft and Nvidia; its founder, CEO and chair – Josh Payne – received total compensation in 2025 of $23.2m, according to last week’s SEC filing notice. Pensions Awareness Ireland launches AI retirement coach

The platform aims to give employees greater clarity and insight when it comes to planning for retirement. As part of its plans for Pensions Awareness Week 2026, Pensions Awareness Ireland has launched what it claims to be Ireland’s first AI retirement coach. The aim of the model, named Ali, is to empower employees to gain greater control and clarity over their financial future. Primarily, it can help workers to see if their pension pot is on track to support the lifestyle they intend to have post-employment. Employees can then explore their options and start to implement any necessary changes they need to make in order to improve their long-term financial outlook. The model will require data such as the user’s current age, their planned retirement age, annual gross income and monthly pension contributions to enable its AI tools to build a dashboard breaking down their retirement income forecast and pension details. From there, dashboard users will receive an invitation to join a conversation with Ali, which has been trained on the Irish pensions system and is programmed to provide tailored responses based on the user’s dashboard. Ali enables people to explore what their forecast means for their own circumstances and the changes they could make in a judgement-free digital space. The aim is to turn retirement planning from something abstract and uncertain into something people can understand, talk about and take action on.” In late June, the Department of Enterprise, Tourism and Employment brought into effect the Employment (Contractual Retirement Ages) Act 2025, which introduces significant new rights for employees in Ireland and also places new legal restrictions and responsibilities on employers. The new rules mean that eligible employees, who wish to do so, can now choose to remain employed at their place of work for a period of time beyond their contractual retirement age, where that age is below the State Pension age of 66. Meta Now Banning Ads That Promote Feminism

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
For years, human rights monitors have been outraged at the selective policing of content on Meta’s social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram. The company’s latest enforcement action is hardly shocking at this point, but it provides a crystal clear example of the kinds of things Meta will — and won’t — allow on its apps. In a bizarre case of digital censorship, Meta’s content moderation system stepped in to stop a local theatre in Barcelona from promoting a stage adaptation on the grounds that it violated a rule on promoting “social topics,” per the Guardian. The production in question is an adaptation of “A Room of One’s Own,” a book by the legendary English author and feminist Virginia Woolf. According to the Teatre Raval, planned promotional posts about the show were blocked on both Instagram and Facebook, likely by an automated content moderation system. After reaching out to Meta about why the promo campaign had been stomped out, the company replied that “any mention of civil rights, feminism or social reform are usually classified as ‘social topics’ by our automatic system,” and therefore rejected. According to the Guardian, the company then cryptically recommended the Raval remove “key words” related to social activism, so the promo campaign can pass the automated censor — though the company failed to explain what, exactly, its content system objected to. “We don’t know if the publicity has been rejected because of the name of Virginia Woolf or because of the word feminism, or both,” Clara Sanchis, who stars in the one-woman show told the Spanish newspaper La Vanguardia, per the Guardian, which translated the comments. “In any case, it’s a way of erasing from the map advances that have taken centuries to achieve,” Sanchis continued. “We have given social media unlimited power and every day we can see how dangerous this is. It’s bias and manipulation.” The automated rejection and the ensuing apathy from Meta come after some galling incidents where Meta refused to take down content significantly more radical than a theatrical performance about a literary figure whose books are read by high schoolers. In August, Futurism reported that Meta was refusing to take meaningful action against a far-right influencer campaign to drum up support for a “crusader march on Dearborn” Michigan, organized by self-described “anti-Arab” extremist Jake Lang. Though Lang’s initial post wasn’t “sponsored,” meaning he didn’t officially pay to promote his call-to-action as the Teatre Raval was trying to do, his post nonetheless garnered thousands of reactions, comments, and shares from his 274,000 followers. It also spawned a deluge of copycat posts extolling racism and Islamophobia, some of which contained explicit calls for violence which Meta refused to take down. And earlier in September, Meta was found to be hosting a massive number of AI-generated videos depicting horrific acts of child abuse. Meta removed a tiny handful of videos after Futurism approached the company for comment — but refused to act on others, leaving the vast majority of violent AI Reels untouched. In all, Meta has drawn a revolting line in the sand — with nominally progressive “social topics” on the verboten side, and base depictions of torture and calls for violence allowed sight unseen. What is clear is that air traffic control is in dire straights across the United States — though the American public has yet to encounter any federal system which a hallucinating, error-prone AI assistant can’t make significantly worse. As backlash against its surveillance network refuses to die down, Flock Safety is kicking off an employee buyout program that’ll let workers leave the company with a “generous” severance package, Wired reports. Protestors have targeted Flock’s AI-powered license plate scanning cameras so fiercely that many cities and police departments are cancelling or declining to extend their contracts, which could leave the enterprise short of revenue goals, according to Wired‘s sources. It’s either buyouts for the 1,500 worker-strong company, or layoffs. Employees who want to leave are getting a solid deal, at least according to Flock. The voluntary severance program, which started accepting applications Friday, gives employees several months of health care coverage and the ability to exercise stock options within two years of separation. Some employees have reportedly said they’ve received buyout offers in the tens of thousands of dollars. Sources familiar with the program said they expected that a significant number of employees will leave. It’s not just the overwhelming backlash against the company that could push employees to exit. There are also rumors that the company might sell off parts or all of its business. “When you see enough of the vandalism,” one of the people told Wired, “you know the writing is on the wall.” An analysis by the advocacy group Secure Justice found that at least 214 cities and counties have dropped their Flock contracts since 2021 — and 90 of those cancelled or lapsed contracts came in 2026 alone. In August, an average of three local administrations were cancelling their Flock contracts each day. Because of the purpose of Flock’s services — conducting mass surveillance — these cancellations have created a vicious cycle. The more municipalities that its cameras go down in, the less useful that Flock becomes to its remaining customers. Some police departments have explicitly cited the loss of coverage from neighboring towns cancelling their contracts as a reason to do the same. When Flock was confronted with the cancellation data less than a month ago, a spokesperson confidently responded that renewals were outpacing cancellations. But if the buyout program is any indication, there’s a lot more anxiety about its future behind the scenes. More on privacy: Cops Are Giving Complete Nonsense Reasons to Search Thousands of Flock Cameras
Futurism
Bloomberg Finds That the AI Industry’s Finances Are a “Wobbly House of Cards”
2026-09-21

Bloomberg Finds That the AI Industry’s Finances Are a “Wobbly House of Cards”

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
While the majority of AI leaders seem to agree that we need to take the foot off the AI development pedal or else risk disaster, Wall Street isn’t exactly enthralled by the idea. For years now, analysts have warned that the United States economy is massively overindexed on AI and resting on a staggering amount of debt, with enormous data center projects and eye-bulging capital expenditures fueling fears over a growing bubble. And now that most major frontier AI labs are looking to slow things down, investors are ringing the alarm over a “wobbly house of cards,” as Bloomberg reports. If this house of cards were to collapse, they warn, the consequences could be devastating. While it’s an already-familiar refrain at this point, it simply — and perhaps fortunately — hasn’t been tested just yet. But the longer we wait, the more dire the outcome, as the stakes continue to grow at an alarming pace. “People may not fully grasp just how wound up the market and the economy is in all of this,” Callodine Capital Management CEO Jim Morrow told Bloomberg. “There are just so many things to unravel if it starts.” According to the latest estimates, AI-related spending is currently accounting for roughly half of the United States’ GDP growth. Without it, the country could spiral into a financial crisis on the scale of the dot-com crash. Meanwhile, tech leaders, including Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, his counterpart at OpenAI, Sam Altman, and even Elon Musk, argue that we need to slow down AI development in light of what they perceive to be an existential threat. But whether that’s even an option remains debatable, per Bloomberg. For one, the world’s other biggest AI economy, China, is laughing at at the very idea, dismissing the remarks as “fear-mongering.” “If we see AI development slow, that means capex is likely to slow,” Ameriprise chief market strategist Anthony Saglimbene told the magazine. “Any slowdown would reset the profit expectations for the entire ecosystem. Given how concentrated the market is to AI, that would be a severe headwind.” Rising interest rates could raise the stakes even further, making it even more expensive for companies to borrow money to cover their enormous projected expenditures. Then there’s the simple fact that nobody knows if any of this spending will pay off in the long run. A return on investment is little more than a distant and tentative dream in 2026 — and open source AI systems are now constantly nipping at the heels of frontier models, making a profitable business model feel hazier than ever. If the house of cards were to collapse, some companies would probably survive while countless others fold. “Will there be winners? Absolutely,” Boston Partners director of global market research Michael Mullaney told Bloomberg. “It’s hard to say who is going to wind up on the other side of this thing and coining money to justify all their expenses.” “There will be, but it’s not going to be a boatload of companies, it’s going to be a handful of companies,” he added. OpenAI forms math advisory group as its AI resolves more than 100 open problems

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. On Monday, OpenAI announced a new independent advisory group hosted at the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton, New Jersey. Called the Advisory Group on Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence, the group is meant to give mathematicians more input into the company’s math-oriented research. “This group will serve as a bridge to the mathematical community and broader public, giving mathematicians a voice in how we move forward,” the post reads. The announcement comes after the abrupt publication of a solution to the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem. As part of the announcement of the new group, OpenAI also claims that the same internal model has resolved more than 100 additional open problems across most areas of mathematics. Many prominent mathematicians have taken issue with the frenzied pace of those results. Earlier this month, 25 Fields Medal-winning mathematicians signed an open letter arguing that AI labs are threatening their intellectual work as they seek to one-up each other with solutions to famous math problems. True to its name, OpenAI’s new group will mostly serve in an advisory capacity, assessing the significance of new results and coordinating their release. While members won’t be paid, they can offer unsolicited advice, go public with their views, and control their own membership, giving them a measure of independence from the company. However, the group won’t be given leeway to slow down or redirect OpenAI’s ongoing mathematical research. As the blog post puts it, “the group will not be responsible for advising us on how to pace our internal progress on mathematics.” The Institute for Advanced Study emphasized this point in its own announcement. “Although we will give advice, we do not have decision making power at any AI company, and the responsibility for the decisions made by any company will rest with that company,” said the institute in a press release. Nine prominent mathematicians have been named as initial members of the group. Notably, only one of the members — IAS’s Camillo De Lellis — also signed the Fields Medalists’ letter. Meta’s Muse is outpacing ChatGPT’s early mobile launch
2026-09-21

Meta’s Muse is outpacing ChatGPT’s early mobile launch

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Meta’s latest AI app, Muse, could be the social giant’s next major hit, new data suggests. According to just- released estimates from market intelligence provider Apptopia, Muse’s app on mobile devices has been downloaded more times during its first 12 days on the market than ChatGPT was in the 12 days after its debut. (This data compares the U.S. and Canada markets only.) Muse also has more daily active users than ChatGPT had at the time, the firm found. Until now, it had been difficult to make an exact apples-to-apples comparison between these two apps, given that they had pursued different launch strategies. When ChatGPT arrived on mobile, it was made available globally, but only on iOS. Muse, meanwhile, is available across both Apple’s App Store and Google Play, but only in the U.S. and Canada. To better align the numbers for comparison, Apptopia looked only at the iOS data for the U.S. and Canada for both apps during the first 12 days of their respective launches. In this subset of the data, Muse has now seen 1.8 million downloads to ChatGPT’s 1.3 million. Overall, Muse has seen 2.8 million total installs globally in its first 12 days, the firm also said. Its growth hasn’t yet stagnated, either; Muse has moved up from its original position as No. 2 overall on the U.S. App Store immediately after its launch to now No. 1, as Business Insider reported on Friday. That jump put the app higher than ChatGPT, the outlet noted. Another firm, Appfigures, said at the time that Muse had then crossed 1 million downloads. In addition, Apptopia’s data indicates that Muse’s U.S. daily active users are now higher than they were for ChatGPT at the same point after its launch. When comparing just the U.S. mobile app daily active users, Muse comes in higher with 642,000 daily users compared with 231,000 for ChatGPT at the time. To be fair to the fact that Muse is available across both iOS and Android, while ChatGPT launched on iOS only, Apptopia narrowed the comparison to iOS alone. Yet, even here, Muse is coming in higher, with 359,000 daily active users on iOS, still above ChatGPT’s figures from that time period. Apptopia can only provide third-party estimates about an app’s downloads and active users; it doesn’t have direct access to Meta’s internal figures. But even if these numbers are only correct from a general “ballpark” perspective, they indicate that Muse could have a shot at becoming Meta’s newest top app. Meta already perfected its cross-promotion strategy when it launched Instagram’s Threads, which now has 500+ million users, thanks to heavy marketing and integrations in Meta’s largest apps, including Instagram and Facebook. Muse is likely to get a similar push, given that the AI app can connect with both of those platforms. It also works inside WhatsApp, so that could give it another boost. While Apptopia doesn’t have visibility into Meta’s cross-promotion efforts or ads directly, it did note that over 95% of Muse’s users are also Facebook users and 63% are Instagram users. Meta, which has been asked for comment, has not yet shared public figures related to Muse’s early adoption. Correction: An earlier version of this article referenced the analytics firm Appfigures as a source for data from Apptopia. This has been fixed. Kairos Power gets up to $100M from Samsung group to build nuclear reactor for Google
2026-09-21

Kairos Power gets up to $100M from Samsung group to build nuclear reactor for Google

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Kairos Power has picked engineering firm Samsung C&T to help build its 50-megawatt demonstration reactor for Google, which the nuclear startup is aiming to have completed by 2030. As part of the deal, Samsung C&T will make an equity investment in Kairos and provide “in-kind” engineering services, the combination of which is worth up to $100 million, Kairos Power said Monday. The equity investment will make up $70 million of the total, a Kairos spokesperson told TechCrunch. Samsung C&T has built or helped build about a dozen nuclear reactors around the globe. Kairos is part of a wave of nuclear startups that have benefited from the surge in power demand from AI data centers. Google first signed a deal with the company in fall 2024, calling on Kairos to build nuclear reactors capable of generating about half a gigawatt of electricity by 2035. Currently, Kairos is building two reactors in Oak Ridge, Tennessee. The first, Hermes 1, is a low-power demonstrator that will help the company refine its commercial design. The second, Hermes 2, is Kairos’ first commercial-scale reactor. Output from Hermes will count as the first 50 megawatts of its deal with Google. The startup received approval from the Nuclear Regulatory Commission in late 2024 to build the reactors. It expects Hermes 2 to begin operating in 2030, in line with its original agreement with Google. Hermes 2 will be a fluoride salt-cooled high-temperature reactor, a design that has been floated for years but never built at commercial scale. Fluoride salts have high boiling points, which will help keep pressure levels low. That, in turn, will limit the risk of high-pressure blowouts in case of any parts failures. Kairos is also using a relatively new form of nuclear fuel known as TRISO, which encapsulates tiny seeds of uranium fuel in layers of ceramic and carbon, which are then loaded into billiard ball-sized spheres. The arrangement is intended to prevent meltdowns. The startup has about five years to get its first power plant up and running, which is an accelerated but manageable timeline in the nuclear industry. To fulfill the terms of its deal with Google, Kairos will need to move even faster on subsequent reactors, something Samsung C&T might be able to help with. Sunday night, users of Meta’s AI assistant Muse started getting a strange error message when they tried to buy goods on Amazon. As spotted by GeekWire, the error message read: “Continued access by an unauthorized AI agent violates Amazon’s Conditions of Use, to which our customers have agreed.” In other words, Muse isn’t welcome as a shopper — and anyone trying to buy something on Amazon through the AI agent will have to find it somewhere else. It’s easy to see the block as a tête-à-tête between tech giants. Amazon has its own cohort of foundation models, after all, along with one of the most popular inference platforms on the internet. As long as they’re under no legal obligation to open the doors to Muse, why would they? But there are also substantive reasons Amazon might not want to be in the agentic commerce business just yet. If Muse makes a bad order, Amazon is going to be the one to clean it up — dealing with both the angry customer and the angry vendor. Muse has one of the lower hallucination rates, as AI models go, but it’s still pretty far from zero. Even if Amazon sees an opportunity in agent-driven commerce, they may want to wait a few more release cycles before rolling anything out. Last day to book an exhibit table is September 18. X will now tell users when governments have forced it to limit their posts
2026-09-21

X will now tell users when governments have forced it to limit their posts

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. X on Monday announced it will now tell users when their posts have been “shadowbanned,” meaning shown to fewer people, due to the company’s compliance with local laws. The feature is an expansion of the social network’s earlier transparency tool called “Under the Hood,” which allows users to see if any labels have been applied to their account or their posts that could have affected their visibility on the platform. It will also highlight any downranking of users’ posts due to government-required content filtering. We’ve expanded Under The Hood after seeing people’s appreciation of more transparency into government-required content filtering.In addition to showing labels applied to your account and posts in the prior month, Under The Hood now shows if your account or posts had their… https://t.co/Op5rBnE1jg pic.twitter.com/ZUrbkO28ay Previously, information about how an X user’s account or posts were being labeled and downranked was not available to end users. As a result, many often accused the company, formerly known as Twitter, of having shadowbanned them. In addition, most companies that share data about government requests do so on an aggregate basis through “transparency reports,” not at the account or post level. That began to change when, in August, X rolled out its transparency tool to offer more visibility into its moderation decisions. The “Under the Hood” option in the X app settings allows anyone who has posted at least 10 times during the month to download their aggregate stats as a JSON file, a standard data format. (Non-technical users can drop the file into any AI chatbot to have it explained.) This same process will now show if an account or posts have had their visibility limited because X is complying with local laws. The file will also include which posts were withheld in a country following a legal demand, and which country made the request. Governments cite a range of reasons for such demands, from laws against hate speech to terrorism-related content to election rules. Of course, critics, including free-expression groups, say some countries also use these laws to squash political dissent. The update follows a number of other transparency-related efforts at X, including the open-sourcing of its “For You” algorithm, which selects the posts in users’ main feed, and an expansion of its efforts to open source parts of its codebase. The latter involved adding more detail, including on the model configuration, filtering, and core ranking system details. That means the code now includes the parameters used to weight different signals — information that is key to understanding which posts are actually displayed. At the time of that open-sourcing effort, X’s vice president of product, Keith Coleman, said it was “the kind of thing that I think people will be fairly shocked that we are releasing.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Trump and Iran Exchange Threats as Leaders Converge for Key U.N. Gathering. Can the Meeting Cool Rising Tensions?
2026-09-21

Trump and Iran Exchange Threats as Leaders Converge for Key U.N. Gathering. Can the Meeting Cool Rising Tensions?

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Law enforcement secures an entrance to the United Nations during the start of the General Assembly in New York City on September 21, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Delegations from the U.S. and Iran are traveling to New York for the world’s largest diplomatic gathering this week in what experts say could be a key moment for determining the direction of the Iran war nearly seven months in. Tensions between the warring countries are mounting ahead of the United Nations General Assembly (UNGA), with Iranian leaders and President Donald Trump exchanging a fresh round of threats. Trump on Sunday weighed whether he should “blow the entire nation up” in an interview with Fox News, warning that Iran “better behave” and saying that “very big things” would happen in the war, without offering more detail. According to the outlet, Trump described his options as “wiping Iran out, letting them rot economically, or making a deal.” A spokesperson for Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), Hossein Mohebbi, responded with a warning that if the U.S. were to launch fresh attacks against Iran, Tehran would use “new weapons and capabilities,” according to Iran's Fars news agency. “If a new aggression takes place, we will definitely change the weapons and geography of the war,” Mohebbi said. The threats come after active hostilities in the conflict resumed in the last month when the U.S. and Iran traded fresh strikes amid tensions over the Strait of Hormuz, a key waterway in the global oil market. Meaningful negotiations between the two countries have meanwhile been stalled for weeks following the breakdown of a memorandum of understanding leaders signed in June. And Yemen’s Iranian-backed Houthi rebels have further shaken the Gulf region by launching attacks against Saudi Arabia and capturing Perim Island, a strategic foothold in the Bab El-Mandeb Strait, another vital maritime trade route in the region. Read More: What Is the Status of the U.S.-Iran Peace Talks? Here's What Both Sides Are Saying Experts tell TIME that the U.N. General Assembly gathering, where delegations from Iran, the U.S., and key Gulf states will be in attendance, could offer a critical chance to deescalate the situation and attempt to revive peace efforts—although making progress in negotiations could be an uphill battle. “This week in New York, there is an opportunity, especially for the mediating countries, to try to prevent tensions from once again spiraling, and potentially this time spiraling out of control,” says Ali Vaez, the deputy program director for the Middle East and North Africa at the International Crisis Group. “It's the last best opportunity to try to prevent an escalation either in October or in November.” Top officials from the U.S. and Iran will be present at the U.N. meeting this week, including Trump, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, and U.S. ambassador to the U.N. Mike Waltz. Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi will be in attendance as part of a slimmed-down delegation from the country. Both Trump and Pezeshkian are set to deliver addresses to the assembly. Vaez says, however, that any effort to revive peace talks between the two countries during the event will largely rely on mediation by Gulf states. Trump is set to hold talks with leaders from the Gulf Cooperation Council—a regional alliance including Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Qatar, Kuwait, Oman, and Bahrain—on the sidelines of the gathering in the coming days. Sources with knowledge of the anticipated meeting told Axios, which first reported the news, that discussions are expected to concern next steps in the war with Iran, with a focus on U.S. ideas for a strategy after the conflict concludes. Vaez believes that the Qataris could play a leading role in mediation efforts, with the support of countries such as Saudi Arabia, Oman, Turkey, Pakistan, and Egypt. “It all depends on whether the mediating countries would be able to find a mutually acceptable formula,” he says of the possibility of the U.S. and Iran coming to some kind of agreement. “Because at this moment, both sides are in a zero-sum situation in which there is literally no overlap between what the Iranians demand and what the U.S. is seeking to achieve.” Given what he characterized as maximalist demands from both the U.S. and Iran, Vaez contends that “some sort of a sweetener,” or incentive, must be given to each side for a deal to be reached, which he says may come in the form of revisions to the now-expired memorandum of understanding. Sanam Valki, director of the Middle East and North Africa Programme at the policy institute Chatham House, agrees that it is far more likely that Gulf states mediate between Iran and the U.S. But, she adds, a direct exchange between U.S. and Iranian delegations is also a possibility—albeit a very slim one. “The big question out there is: Will there be some sort of surprise encounter between the Iranians and the U.S.?” Valki says. Trump told Fox News’ Trey Yingst on Sunday that he would be willing to meet with Pezeshkian during the U.N. gathering. Beyond either mediated or direct discussions during the event, another potential outcome of this week’s General Assembly meeting is a United Nations Security Council Resolution regarding the Iran conflict, Vaez says. Unlike a memorandum of understanding like the one the U.S. and Iran previously reached, a security resolution would be legally binding by international law and would compel both parties to abide by certain conditions in the war. Adopting a resolution would require the votes of at least nine out of the 15 members of the security council. And as a complicating factor, the U.S.—as well as the other four permanent members—would have the ability to defeat any such measure by casting a veto. “This is a very important week at UNGA for regional states, as well as the Iranians themselves, to try and roll back or manage how things evolve,” Valki tells TIME. “The President is very clearly weighing and considering the military options that are on his desk, which include not just responding to the Houthis, but another round of serious and severe strikes against the Islamic Republic.” If a direct exchange between the U.S. and Iranian delegations does unexpectedly occur during the gathering, she says, “it's a big deal. It's a big outcome. It means that the Iranian president is willing to risk his standing at home, attacks at home, in order to stave off a serious military attack and build a direct lifeline to the Trump team.” Up until now, both sides have sought “maximalist outcomes with minimal concessions,” Valki says. But Iran, through direct conversation with the U.S. delegation, could “signal a more transformational type of policy outreach, rather than the defensive reaction of traditional hardliners in the country.” On Trump’s end, Vaez says the President could potentially make concessions to Iran by removing naval blockades on Iranian ports, rolling back sanctions against Tehran, or returning access to certain frozen assets. “That's what the President can put on the table … The most important thing for Iranians is economic reprieve and that's precisely where President Trump has tremendous leverage.” A security council resolution, meanwhile, “could provide a degree of assurance about the sustainability of a new understanding between Iran and the U.S.,” Vaez says. He adds that through such a resolution, the U.N. could also establish an “observer mission”—what he describes as “an established mechanism that could look into disputes and adjudicate” if there are disagreements over alleged violations of the resolution, as there were previously over June’s memorandum of understanding. Vaez stresses, though, that the odds of this week’s gathering resulting in a meaningful breakthrough in the stalled negotiations appears unlikely. “There are very serious differences, and there's deep mistrust,” he says. “And so that's why I'm not very optimistic.”

Why Older Americans Are Drinking More Alcohol—Not Less
2026-09-21

Why Older Americans Are Drinking More Alcohol—Not Less

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The U.S. is in the midst of a temperance boom. Drinking rates have been steadily declining nationwide, with an August Gallup poll showing that only 54% of Americans ever consume alcohol—sharply down from 67% as recently as 2010. Now, a study in the Annals of Internal Medicine takes those findings a step further, reporting that younger adults in the Millennial and Gen Z cohorts—people born from 1981 to 2012—are driving that trend. Meanwhile, older Americans, aged 50-64, are actually consuming more alcohol than they used to, with rates increasing by more than one third since 2018. “It’s encouraging to see alcohol use decline for the first time since the start of the pandemic,” said lead author Dr. Brian Lee, a hepatologist and liver transplant specialist with Keck Medicine of USC, in a statement that accompanied the release of the report. “However, use remains above pre-pandemic levels and certain groups continue to have concerning trends.” Lee and his collaborators conducted their work by sifting through the responses from more than 114,000 U.S. adults who, from 2018 to 2024, participated in the National Health Interview Survey—an annual study conducted by the U.S. Centers of Disease Control and Prevention. The survey consists of several face-to-face interviews throughout the year. In the current study, researchers looked at whether and how much respondents drink; heavy drinking, for instance, is defined as five or more drinks per day or 16 or more per week for males, and four or more drinks per day or eight or more drinks per week for females. Americans saw a 2% drop in overall drinking and 8% in heavy drinking in just the 2022 to 2024 window. Total drinking dropped most dramatically among Gen Z, with rates falling nearly 6% from 2022 to 2024, compared with about 2% for Millennials. Heavy drinking fell by roughly a fifth in both generations. Older Americans, however, went in the opposite direction, with those in the 50 to 64 age group seeing an increase of 4% in overall drinking, and 36% spike in heavy drinking from 2018 to 2024. The reasons for the changes are not clear, but greater public awareness of the health risks of drinking may play a role in alcohol’s overall decline. Alcohol has been linked to 62 different diseases, according to the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, especially liver disease, cardiovascular disease, and various forms of cancer. But not all Americans are equally aware of alcohol’s health harms. Past Research from 2024 found that far more younger people know that alcohol negatively affects health compared to older adults. Some of that knowledge may track back to the COVID-19 pandemic. “There was a lot of messaging around young adults who were dying more from alcohol-related harm during the pandemic,” says Lee. “So they could have been targeted for this.” It’s seniors, however, who could most use the same kind of public information, especially as their drinking rates go up—because the older people get, whether they drink or not, the more likely they are to contract most diseases. “This age group faces some of the greatest risks of harm from alcohol, including cirrhosis and alcohol-related cancers,” Lee said in the statement. Public information campaigns are just one of the possible causes of the drop in drinking among young people. It’s also possible that Gen Z and Millennials are simply swapping one intoxicant for another. “The idea of substitution is a leading theory,” says Lee. “With the legalization of cannabis, are people using that rather than alcohol?” And sometimes, it might simply come down to money—which most older folks have in relative abundance compared to younger people. “Younger adults in general have less disposable income [to spend on alcohol] than middle-aged or older adults,” says Lee. Reducing drinking—or cutting it out altogether—may ultimately be a self-reinforcing habit, as social expectations shift in a newly temperate demographic. “When fewer people around you drink, there is less and less societal pressure,” says Lee. “Older adults grew up in an era where everyone was drinking heavily around them and it was acceptable, whereas that's no longer the case among Gen Z or Millennials.” Whatever the causes of our society’s new sobriety, the less alcohol anyone consumes, the better. Young people may have tumbled to that truth first, but the older age groups among us  would benefit from doing the same.

What Russia’s Parliamentary Election Results Mean for Putin and the War

What Russia’s Parliamentary Election Results Mean for Putin and the War

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russian President Vladimir Putin attends a meeting with the head of the Federal Customs Service in Moscow on Sept. 21, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia's electoral commission on Monday said that United Russia, the ruling party aligned with President Vladimir Putin, is projected to win the parliamentary election that has been widely criticized as a “sham.” The Central Election Commission (CEC) reported that 95% of the votes have been counted after a three-day election that began last Friday and ended Sunday. While the final counts are not expected to be certified until later this week, preliminary figures show that the pro-Kremlin party is projected to win a record 355 seats in the country’s 450-seat Duma, the lower house of parliament, giving United Russia an even larger supermajority than its 2016 record high of 343 seats. Read More: Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured Voter turnout was high at just over 59%, according to a briefing from the CEC. The election is the first parliamentary poll since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, and the Kremlin has cast it as a referendum on support for what it calls a “special military operation” rather than a war. It was also, therefore, the first parliamentary election to include voters from four partly occupied regions of Ukraine—Donetsk, Luhansk, Kherson and Zaporizhzhia—which Moscow claimed to annex illegally in 2022 but does not fully control. Voting also was held in Crimea, which was illegally annexed in 2014. Despite the vote being framed as an opportunity for citizens to express sentiments about the war, it is widely considered an illusion of choice: Russia’s supreme court last month banned the federal candidate list of the one registered antiwar party, Yabloko, from participating in the State Duma. “I would be very careful to kind of unpack the distribution of votes as something indicative of what people think of the war,” David Szakonyi, a professor at George Washington University whose research focuses on Putin-era Russia, tells TIME. “They were not given the choice to vote against it.” “This playing field was tilted before the votes were even counted, and this was not by any means a free and fair election,” he adds. Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov told members of the press Monday that the election showed how Russians support the country’s leader and “point to the highest level of consolidation within Russian society around the head of state.” Experts believe that the election will allow the Kremlin to continue justifying its war with Ukraine—and it could further consolidate Putin’s power. United Russia’s supermajority could embolden parliament to pass “drastic legislation” with regard to future war efforts, Szakonyi says. Although United Russia already held a constitutional supermajority in the outgoing Duma, the larger margin could embolden lawmakers to pursue more severe measures. “They have every resource available to them to change the political system as Putin directs,” Szakonyi says, “and the propaganda in the next week or two is going to heavily emphasize the fact that United Russia improved its performance.” Parliament could specifically use the election to request more sacrifices from citizens for the war effort, according to Szakonyi. He says, for example, that the party could support legislation expanding military recruitment or defending “greater investment in the war effort, at the expense of healthcare, education, and domestic spending in general.” “It is highly possible that they will at least kind of build on these electoral results when they demand more from voters and citizens in the next couple months in order to win the war,” he continues. Olena Lennon, a practitioner in residence of National Security at the University of New Haven, agrees that Putin did not necessarily need any more legislative power to exact his objectives. Instead, the election was “more about creating an image of national unity and demonstrating to various audiences that Putin has total political control," she says, adding that hosting an election offers the “optics of popular legitimacy, institutional strength, and political stability.” “That potentially gives Putin greater political cover and legitimacy for unpopular measures in the future—such as putting the economy in wartime production mode and potentially full mobilization," Lennon says. A new mobilization campaign could inject new momentum into Russia’s offensive campaign and replenish battlefield losses. Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said last month that he believes Russia is planning ​to draft an additional 300,000 troops for the war effort. “We are confident that following the elections, they will carry out a mobilization. We believe this will not take place in the major cities,” Zelenskyy said on Aug. 23. “They’ll lose all 300,000 of these newly mobilized Russians there; we’ll wipe them out.” Putin has denied that a new mobilization campaign is planned, calling the rumors “utter nonsense” on Sept. 1. Two days later, at the Eastern Economic Forum in Vladivostok, Putin emphasized that there “is no scenario today that would require mobilization.” More than four years into the war, Russians are facing rising living costs amid inflation that some economists attribute partly to military spending, with the prices of several food staples in rising this year. The country is also experiencing fuel shortages as Ukraine continues to strategically target its oil refineries and depots. U.S. President Donald Trump has criticized the tactic in recent weeks, arguing that it is impacting global fuel prices. Energy-market data over the past six months, however, shows that the ongoing conflict in the Middle East and subsequent constrictions of major oil passageways like the Strait of Hormuz is the much larger disruption to global oil and diesel supplies. Trump’s latest comments on the matter came Monday. “Russia has unfortunately lost control of its Diesel Oil Industry due to its War with Ukraine. A large number of their Diesel refineries have been blown up and are, at least temporarily, out of commission,” Trump said on Truth Social on Sept. 21. “This ridiculous and never ending War with Ukraine must be ended.” Beginning Saturday, as Russia entered its last day of voting, the government said it intercepted more than 1,600 Ukrainian drones, including 450 directed toward Moscow. The figures, attributed to Moscow Mayor Sergey Sobyanin and reported by state media TASS, have not been independently verified. According to a statement by Sobyanin on Telegram, this was the largest ever drone attack on the Russian capital, damaging the city's oil refinery. European powers have largely condemned the results. That includes France's foreign ministry, which released a statement ​on Monday saying that the vote ⁠did not meet the conditions for “free, pluralistic, democratic elections.” “Heightened repression against civil society and the opposition, ⁠the exclusion of several political parties, growing ⁠restrictions on freedom of expression and the banning of independent media have undermined the Russian people's ability to freely form ⁠and express their choice,” the statement said. A joint statement from the United Kingdom, Canada, Norway, Australia, and New Zealand was also released Monday, calling the vote a “sham”—particularly as it pertained to the four occupied Ukrainian territories. “Holding Russia’s State Duma elections in the temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine, as Russia did in Crimea and Donetsk, Kherson, Luhansk and Zaporizhzhia, is a gross violation of Ukraine’s sovereignty and independence, and the UN Charter,” the statement said. “We call on the international community to reject Russia’s attempts to normalise its occupation, condemn the holding of sham elections on Ukrainian territory.” Kaja Kallas, the European Union’s foreign policy chief and a vice president of the European Commission, called the elections “another major setback for Russian democracy” in a post on X. “The Kremlin silenced dissent, barred the only registered party that opposed its war in Ukraine, and shut out international election monitors,” she said. “The veneer of democracy is all that remains in Russia.”

RSV Vaccines Are Highly Effective in Older Adults
2026-09-21

RSV Vaccines Are Highly Effective in Older Adults

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Computer illustration of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In 2023, two new vaccines against respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), which kills thousands of older adults annually in the U.S., hit the market. As millions of people aged 60 and older received the shots, researchers watched and waited to see whether the efficacy numbers seen in clinical trials, which were quite high, would be mirrored in the real world. “There was a little concern that maybe these vaccines will not turn out to be quite as potent as they appeared,” says Dr. Edward Walsh, a professor of medicine at the University of Rochester. The trials had rolled out in the later phases of the COVID-19 pandemic, when there wasn’t as much RSV circulating as usual, which made some researchers wonder how later studies would play out. However, in a study published Sept. 18 in JAMA Network Open looking at 14.8 million seniors, researchers at the U.S. Food and Drug Administration found that the vaccines were highly effective, reducing the chances of an RSV-related hospitalization or death by around 80%. Since the rollout, a handful of studies have looked at the effectiveness of these two vaccines, which are made by GlaxoSmithKline and Pfizer. “These have all—very interestingly, and comfortingly—really paralleled and mimicked the exact results of the efficacy trials,” says Walsh, who was not involved in the new study. But this new work examines the entire U.S. population over 65 who are enrolled in Medicare, which is “vastly greater than the other studies,” he says. So these results are particularly reassuring. In general, both vaccines performed similarly. The study found that people who received an RSV vaccine were more likely to live in affluent areas and more likely to have been vaccinated for flu and COVID, raising the question of whether their higher survival numbers have to do with access to health care. However, when researchers performed an analysis meant to test for whether access to health care was a factor, the high efficacy of the RSV shot remained. “That was nice to see, that the data really was identical,” says Walsh. One of the big questions now is whether and when to give people another shot, says Walsh. Many of the people in the target population received them in the first year they were available, so much so that an RSV vaccine released in 2024, Moderna’s mRNA version, saw less uptake, he says. Would giving people another shot two or three years after their first refresh their immunity to the virus? That’s a subject of active research. The second time an RSV shot is given, “you don't get the same vigorous antibody response that you got when you gave the vaccine for the first time, and whether that is important, I don't think we really know,” Walsh says. (Flu vaccines, by contrast, require annual shots because strains change so much from year to year that this is the best way to get protected.) Researchers will need to explore this in more detail before patients get a recommendation. While this new study confirms that these RSV vaccines protect against the worst outcomes of the virus, work to develop vaccines against other viruses, such as parainfluenza, is ongoing. And there could be more broad-spectrum options available someday. “It may very well be that the ultimate goal would be some sort of a respiratory vaccine that would cover multiple viruses,” Walsh speculates. “One of the thoughts is, well, maybe you can stimulate the innate immune system in a non-specific way such that it would prevent any viral infection.”  Working from the observation that having one virus often protects people from getting another, researchers have been delving into new ways of inducing immunity. “These are the kinds of things that immunologists are thinking about,” he says.

Can ‘Pink Noise’ Help You Sleep Better?
2026-09-21

Can ‘Pink Noise’ Help You Sleep Better?

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When you’re asleep, your brain is still keeping tabs on the world outside. Sounds and even smells have been shown to alter brain activity during sleep. “Evolutionarily, it makes sense,” says Hong-Viet Ngo-Dehning, a lecturer in psychology at the University of Essex in the U.K. “There might be some danger, and we need to respond.” But some sounds might actually nudge us deeper into sleep. In a recent paper in Science Translational Medicine, researchers report that when they played a type of sound called “pink noise” to people sleeping in an fMRI machine, they could see the fluid surrounding the brain pulsing in response. That might indicate that such noises could provoke changes in the quality of sleep. The reason sleep feels restorative is still a subject of active research, but scientists are curious whether the movement of cerebrospinal fluid, which might remove biochemical waste products during sleep, could be involved. “A few years ago, we made this discovery using MRI imaging that when people fall asleep, there are these really large waves of fluid flow that pulse around the brain,” says Laura Lewis, a neuroscientist at MIT and an author of the new study. “Ever since then we've been really interested in understanding what controls these waves…and we think it would be really interesting to see if you could maybe make more.” For this study, Lewis and colleagues drew on prior research using “pink noise,” which is similar to white noise many people use while falling asleep but a bit lower-pitched and more even sounding. “It's less harsh-sounding than static white noise that people might be familiar with,” Lewis says. In 2013, Ngo-Dehning and colleagues found that playing pink noise to people during sleep seemed to improve their performance on memory tasks the next day. In the years since, findings have been mixed, Ngo-Dehning says, but he suspects that this type of noise does do something for the brain, at least under certain conditions. Lewis was curious to see whether bursts of pink noise, which have been shown in previous work to influence a type of brain waves called slow waves, would change brain fluid flow, too. Her coauthor graduate student Joshua Levitt developed an algorithm allowing him to play 50 millisecond bursts of pink noise precisely as slow waves peaked in the brain’s activity, while people were sleeping in an MRI—a tricky proposition, but one that allowed the team to see what the sound did to fluid flow in the brain. (Levitt and Lewis have applied to patent this technology and hope to commercialize it in a product that could be worn during sleep.) What the researchers saw confirmed what others have found: Pink noise amplified slow waves during sleep, potentially making sleep deeper. But they also saw that CSF flow increased in the wake of that amplification. “The most exciting part of our results, at least for me, was that we also observed an increase in cerebrospinal fluid inflow from the fourth ventricle into the brain,” says Levitt. “This was the first example that I'm aware of, at least in humans, of some stimulus being able to modulate cerebrospinal fluid flow non-invasively.” The study is exciting because it shows clearly that pink noise, slow waves, and these pulses of CSF flow are closely linked, says Ngo-Dehning, and it opens the door to studies where researchers intentionally boost CSF flow to see if it can help, say, people with poor sleep or people with neurodegenerative conditions that may be caused by impaired waste removal from the brain. “That would be the next step,” he says.
