CNN, MS NOW, and Politico Sue Trump Administration Over White House Ban

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings CNN, MS NOW, and Politico sued the Trump Administration on Monday following President Donald Trump's decision to ban the three media outlets from the White House. "This morning, we notified the government that we are filing a lawsuit today to protect our First Amendment rights and defend the principle that the government does not decide what the press reports or publishes,” the companies said in a joint statement. Trump took to Truth Social shortly afterwards and insisted the White House "is not instituting an assault on the free press" but is instead targeting "fake news"—something he argued is "totally out of control" and "a threat to our national security." Vice President J.D. Vance, when asked about the lawsuit, argued that Trump is not "banning" media outlets. "What he's doing is saying we're not gonna give you special access to the White House if you engage in what is effectively propaganda," he said. The White House later doubled down on its stance, issuing a press release titled "White House access is a privilege—not a right," during which they argued "the First Amendment protects their right to publish; it does not entitle them to a hard pass, briefing room seat, or place in the press pool." CNN, MS NOW, and Politico said their reporters were denied access to the White House over the weekend and had their badges seized following Trump's ban issued Friday. MS NOW reporter Akayla Gardner on Saturday described arriving at the White House campus to discover her badge no longer worked. "The officer asked me to hand over my badge. He said that it was disabled. I asked why I was not able to get inside. He said it was above him," she recounted. Reporters from CNN and Politico shared similar experiences. “Without notice or process, the White House revoked our journalists’ credentials because it objected to our reporting. Left unchallenged, this threatens press freedom and the public’s right to independent journalism free from government interference," the joint statement from the outlets continued. Trump announced his decision to ban the three media outlets Friday afternoon in a Truth Social post that warned other news outlets could face similar restrictions. “Effective immediately, I am banning Fake News CNN, MSNOW... and Politico... from the White House as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ fake news!” Trump said. “Media Outlets shouldn’t be able to constantly write or report fiction and lies when they’re covering the President of the United States, the Trump Administration, or the United States of America.” When asked in the Oval Office later that day if the media ban is likely to be struck down in court, Trump responded: “I think it's good to point it out whether it survives or doesn't. I don't think that a court should allow fake news to be written day after day after day.” The ban has presented a new test of the First Amendment’s protection of a free press as his Administration moves to punish news organizations whose coverage he dislikes. The First Amendment forbids the government from "abridging" the freedom of the press—a principle intended to allow news organizations to scrutinize those in power without fear of government retaliation over their reporting. CNN is one of five U.S. news outlets in the White House press pool that rotates responsibility for pooled live video coverage. The network was originally due to cover Trump's trip to the U.N. General Assembly in New York on Monday. In light of the ban, the other major television networks are standing with CNN by declining to replace its White House pool crew. The decision means the shared TV pool will not cover Trump’s events that have been designated for pool coverage. In a rare joint statement, ABC, CBS, CNN, Fox News, and NBC said: “The public has a vital interest in receiving accurate, independent information about its government. No Administration should restrict a news organization because it objects to its reporting.” Bryan Boughton, current chair of the TV pool and Fox News Washington bureau chief, had earlier issued a memo to the White House press corps detailing the decision. "This is to advise that, effective today, the TV pool will not be covering events designated as pool coverage of the President,” read the memo, according to CNN. “This follows the White House’s position preventing CNN from fulfilling its assigned pool duties. There will be no replacement pool put in place. All other pool coverage will continue as normal, including Congress, VIPs, and selected events in Washington and around the country.” The Associated Press also sent a note to media late Monday morning, informing them of the changed plans. "Please be advised that no network travel pool is expected to be with U.S. President Donald Trump today as he heads to New York for the United Nations General Assembly," read the notice. In a show of support, some media publications—including the New York Times and Washington Post—announced they would hold off on publishing or distributing still photographs taken as part of the White House pool coverage on Monday until midnight E.T. TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Trump has made several previous attempts to bar or restrict reporters from government spaces. During his first term, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, who was then a CNN correspondent, after a tense exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court and a judge ordered the White House to reinstate his press pass. In early 2025, shortly after returning to office, Trump tried to bar Associated Press journalists from spaces where access is limited, such as the Oval Office and Air Force One, when the news organization declined to update their stylebook after Trump signed an Executive Order renaming the "Gulf of Mexico" as the “Gulf of America.” The Associated Press sued, and the case remains ongoing. The Department of Defense has also imposed various restrictions limiting journalists’ access in the Pentagon. In an unprecedented move in June, the department designated its press office a classified area. “The Pentagon Press Office has been redesignated as a Sensitive Compartmented Information Facility due to speechwriters from the Office of the Secretary of War sharing the facility,” said deputy Pentagon press secretary Joel Valdez. “As a result, journalists will no longer be permitted to enter the office space. There’s nothing controversial about that.” Trump has also verbally lashed out at several female reporters. Last month, CNN issued a statement in defense of its reporter Kristen Holmes, after Trump told her to "be quiet" and called her a "loud, boisterous person" during a tense exchange in the Oval Office. The White House's Rapid Response social media account later shared a clip of the exchange, referring to Holmes as “a disgraceful, humiliating embarrassment to her alleged profession.” In its response to the rhetoric, CNN said: “Public officials are free to challenge reporting they disagree with, but personal attacks on journalists for asking questions are beneath the office and inconsistent with the principles of a free press."
The Atlantic
The Press Is Not the Root of Trump’s Problems
2026-09-21

The Press Is Not the Root of Trump’s Problems

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. President Trump is going through a rough patch—in foreign relations, in the economy, in the courts, and in election outlook—and he believes that he has found the culprits: CNN, MS NOW, and Politico. On Friday, Trump said he was banning the three outlets from the White House “as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS” and warned that more bans could come. When he feels beleaguered, he tends to lash out at familiar enemies, especially reporters. The idea that negative coverage is to blame for his predicament is perversely flattering. But Trump’s true scourge is reality, not how the news is covering it. Although the decision is part of a gradually intensifying attack on free speech, as I noted last month, it has drawn particular pushback. This morning, five major TV networks (including Fox News) agreed to suspend pool coverage of the president and White House events after CNN was blocked from its previously assigned spot in a pool rotation today. The three banned outlets also sued over their exclusion. How such a suit will fare is unclear; Trump banned the Associated Press from the White House press pool last year after it refused to adopt his renaming of the Gulf of Mexico, and a lawsuit challenging the ban is still pending. After Trump committed a Kinsley gaffe on Friday by calling his move “the ban on the free press,” he tried to clean up his spin this morning. “The White House is not instituting an assault on the Free Press, something which I cherish. It is instituting an assault on the FAKE NEWS,” he wrote, claiming that it “is a threat to our National Security, and must be stopped, NOW!” This is nonsense. Trump has every right to hate the way he’s covered, but if the government has the power to decide what is fake and what is true, then the press is not free. Trump’s post demonstrates his talismanic belief in the power of news coverage. The president himself has been obsessed with attention—how to get it and how to shape it—for his entire life, and he is a relic from an era when the media were so dominant, and had such control over the means of reaching an audience, that they seemed to shape reality. They are much diminished today, occupying a strange, unstable role: strong enough to be blamed for both Trump’s rise and his downfall, yet weak enough that they can be threatened with relative impunity. Though Trump’s attacks on free speech are unlike any in living memory, his is not the first administration to believe that its problems are about messaging and coverage. In 2012, Barack Obama said that the biggest mistake of his first term “was thinking that this job was just about getting the policy right,” because “the nature of this office is also to tell a story to the American people.” A decade later, Joe Biden’s administration was convinced that negative coverage of inflation and Biden’s age were bigger problems than inflation or the president’s evident frailty. Now Trump is making the same misjudgment. He is not unpopular because of Politico, an outlet that focuses largely on Washington machinations, or because of MS NOW or CNN, two cable channels that—as he is fond of pointing out—have relatively small audiences for television. The media cannot be “failing” and all-powerful at the same time. Trump is unpopular because (to name a few big reasons) he is losing a war of choice to Iran, making inflation worse without seeming to care how voters feel, overreaching with draconian immigration enforcement, and focusing on vanity projects that don’t matter to voters. Ironically, Trump is committing the same error as many liberal critics of the press, who seem to believe that popular support for the president would have collapsed long ago if the media had used harsher words to describe him. But the idea that tougher coverage would have stopped him is fantasy. Although the press has made many mistakes in how it has reported on the president, including giving him hours of uninterrupted TV air time in 2016, downplaying bigoted remarks, and “sanewashing” some statements, Trump has also received more intensive investigation and negative coverage than any president in American history. Some of that coverage has surely been hackish partisanship—which is protected by the First Amendment too—but much of it has been earned. Newspapers, magazines, wire services, and TV networks have turned out a series of scoops about the chaos inside the administration, Trump’s policy errors, and the pervasive corruption around him. In late 2023, The Atlantic published a set of prescient essays considering what would happen if Trump won back the White House. Voters had even lived through one Trump presidency already. They had access to the relevant information before they voted him in again. Believing that Americans might have chosen differently if only The New York Times had abandoned its obtuse headline conventions is comforting to some Trump critics, because it puts the blame on the press rather than acknowledging that a majority of those who voted wanted Trump. By the same token, Trump would rather blame news outlets he does not like than confront the reality that some of those same voters are rejecting him because of his choices. The president faces an additional risk in trying to ban these three outlets. He detests adversarial coverage, but he desperately desires attention. This afternoon, Trump spoke with reporters outside the White House. Usually, his comments are captured by the boom mics from the pool TV team, but without their presence today, he was inaudible. Censorship can silence anyone, it turns out—even the censor. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: When I was 20 years old, I started to sense a hole inside me. The feeling was utterly concrete, though the result of no medical condition that any of the various doctors I saw could identify. The hole felt like a viscous emptiness—a paradox, admittedly, as any real emptiness should have felt like nothing. This was a nothing, but its nothingness very much felt like something—and something that was growing … My friends found my behavior disturbing. I was missing class in college, showing up to theater rehearsals drunk. A caring young woman I was dating could take no more of my savage, ranting mood swings and persistent despair. She broke up with me over tea at the student union and implored me to seek help. I knew she was right. The Confrontation That Broke New York
2026-09-21

The Confrontation That Broke New York

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
In the spring of 2023, a young Black man stepped onto a subway car in Manhattan. He screamed, threw garbage at passengers, and announced that he was prepared to die. New Yorkers often encase themselves in psychological armor on the subway, but for many of the riders in that car, this was too much. Terror flooded in. A woman hid her face in a friend’s chest; a mother tried to hide behind her stroller with her toddler. One man began to pray. Then a 24-year-old white man, an ex-Marine, grabbed the screaming man and put him in a neck lock, and five minutes later—by mistake? Intentionally?—left that man dead on the floor of the subway car. To read the new book on that killing, Man in the Mirror: Hope, Struggle, and Belonging in an American City, by Anand Giridharadas, is to recognize that the membrane separating safety and fear on the New York City subways appeared in danger of rupturing by 2023. In the preceding few years, dozens of people had been pushed onto subway tracks, suffering injury or death. Murder and assault had risen sharply. The tension between chaos and order—often evident in New York—could be acute. And in those five minutes, it snapped. Before anyone could sort out the facts, too many people rushed to impose narratives. Jordan Neely became the homeless Black man with the too-disposable life; Daniel Penny became the muscular vigilante who grew up in the white-bread suburbs. Leftist activists howled racism; right-wing sorts adjusted their ties and talked tough. “In a civilized society, the strong protect the weak,” a member of The Wall Street Journal’s editorial board wrote. “A Marine uses his strength to fight for those unable to fight for themselves.” Liberal politicians divined morality tales. Brad Lander, at the time the city comptroller, who lives in a prosperous liberal precinct in Brooklyn, proclaimed: “We must not become a city where a mentally ill human being can be choked to death by a vigilante without consequence.” I wanted to say: Stop, please. Forgo reading your political catechisms. Try not to talk of consequences and militarization and the menace of the streets until we have a clue about how to prevent this from happening again. There is pathos enough to be found in basic facts. In his book, Giridharadas takes on the considerable challenge of telling a story that encompasses conflicting narratives and the voices of nearly every player: defense attorneys, people living on the street, public-health officials, both Neely and Penny. His writing is most powerful when he traces the threads of tragically entwined lives. Whereas some in the media reduced Neely to a trope—the mentally ill homeless man—Man in the Mirror invests his life with complication and nuance. His mother was killed by her boyfriend; in a gothic twist, that boyfriend cross-examined young Jordan while representing himself at his trial. In time, Neely drifted further and further away from those who wanted to offer him succor. The reader learns about outreach workers and prosecutors who were ambivalent on how to treat his illness, and about well-meaning bureaucrats whose actions fell short. Read: The heart of the debate over Jordan Neely’s death Here and there, the author stumbles when inserting liberal punctuation points. He argues that addressing crime is an inescapable fact of life for Democrats, even if the fear that crime is out of control is “based in nonreality.” The biggest problem, he writes, is a nation “awash in anxiety and fear: of change, of the social advancement of marginalized populations, of losing control of children, of losing control of the border, of losing cultural status and centrality, of the Other, of chaos.” This strikes me as too easy. Change, demographic and otherwise, has swept through and revitalized New York City for centuries. And the subways are arteries that give life to us all. I grew up riding the rails to see girlfriends and basketball games. My schoolmates and I rode to a Bronx public high school in train cars so encased in graffiti, we had to peer out of the opening doors to see if we’d reached our stop. Slowly, however, that system grew cleaner and safer. When the coronavirus pandemic overtook New York City, that momentum seemed to reverse, and the subways began to feel menacing again. My claim here is reality-based, drawing on statistics and personal experience. Assaults on the subway increased by 65 percent from 2020 to 2025, and the number of murders in the system spiked as well. And a year after Neely died, a man shoved a friend of my family onto the subway tracks. He suffered multiple fractures, and his rehabilitation stretched across brutal months. One morning in the spring of 2023, I was riding the Q train to the Upper East Side when I heard a booming voice and saw a great block of a man enter the subway car and ask for money. I returned to my reading. As the man approached, the timbre of his voice grew agitated. I fixed a side eye on him. When the train stopped in the middle of a tunnel, the panhandler stood over a young man who sat across from me. He put out a massive hand. “You’re giving me money,” he announced. Silence followed as the young passenger kept eyes downcast. “Mutherfucker, I’ve got a big-ass knife”—the man patted his shaggy overcoat—“and I’m going to gut you from your balls to your throat.” I made eye contact with other men on the train. What the hell do we do if he pulls out that knife? If we are lucky enough to pull him to the floor, how do we keep him there? I offer none of this to sound macho. I’m a 60-something man with a strong desire to remain alive. I harbored no illusions about my ability to wrestle this man to the ground. As it happened, the train started and pulled into the next station and he got off, but not before loudly vowing to gut us all. I can venture a reasonable thumbnail sketch of this man. He was probably homeless. He was likely known to social-services caseworkers and to police. If he had been prescribed medications, he did not seem to be taking them. His inner resources had been eroded by life’s dysfunctions and the brutal reality of too many days on the street. In a 2024 column about Neely and Penny in The New York Times, Emmett Rensin noted that the prevalence of “madness”—“those serious and spectacular forms of mental illness”—on the streets and in the subways “creates a dilemma between two principles of a liberal society: the sovereignty of individuals and the collective right to public order.” When those values can’t be reconciled, the city can feel perilous. I have a desperate desire not to live in a Death Wish version of New York, in which residents take it upon themselves to mete out justice, but what happens when you are caught in a moral pincer without a third party to adjudicate? Had that man on the Q pulled a knife, and had we hurt him before he hurt us, would we have been branded vigilantes? The worst of those times appear to have passed. Murders and shootings in New York City have plunged in the past few years, and it remains one of the nation’s safest big cities. But statistics comfort only to a point. The continuing presence in public of so many people with seemingly untreated mental illness—including those who still push strangers onto the tracks—is unignorable. To read Man in the Mirror is to inoculate yourself against the belief that the larger crisis is over. Giridharadas’s book illuminates the conundrum posed by those with Neely’s behavioral profile. On a good day, he could be a gentle busker. He was the Michael Jackson impersonator with a moonwalk step and a sweet smile. But Neely’s mental illness most often went untreated, and it had festered as he evaded caseworkers. He was becoming dangerous. In 2019, he punched someone on a subway platform. Two years later, he slugged a 67-year-old woman as she emerged from a subway station, breaking her nose and fracturing her eye socket; for this, he served a sentence at Rikers Island. Locking away the Neelys of our cities in Kafkaesque asylums was a more common solution until the 1960s and ’70s; the practice was ultimately rejected for urgent and humane reasons. Yet discerning which mentally ill person poses a threat has not gotten any easier, and letting the most afflicted roam the streets untreated does not constitute a sustainable policy. Giridharadas writes sensitively about this, and illuminates debates between hopeful public-health officials who yearn to transform society—changing “everything ‘out there’”—and hardheaded pragmatists. The latter group supports a sensible program in New York, now in its early stages, to pair public caseworkers with police officers, marrying the compassion of the former with the practical authority of law enforcement—the better to offer the right kind of help in any situation. There is also an effort to make it easier for families to commit mentally ill relatives for care. Unfortunately, when Giridharadas moves away from the man who died in the confrontation and the questions that arise from the incident, his analysis grows less subtle. He devotes substantial pages early in his account to the Black activist Al Sharpton without mentioning the extent to which the reverend is a sometimes-unreliable narrator. Sharpton can speak movingly of the lives of the Black forgotten and of those who suffer from police abuse; he also has served as an FBI informant, been on a first-name basis with mobsters, and embraced an outright hoax about a racist conspiracy. And in his politics, he is in some ways an outlier. Many Black New Yorkers, as well as Latinos and Asians, have told pollsters that they favor more, not less, police presence, and that safer subways are an urgent concern. That perspective could have enriched the book. The author also succumbs in places to class-based stereotypes, particularly when writing of Penny and his family of white suburbanites, people who are unlikely to share his political priors. Such missteps limit rather than expand our understanding of Penny’s background and worldview. Penny grew up in West Islip, on Long Island, a town where, Giridharadas writes, “you had your little yard, your little fence, your little deck, maybe you had your little boat.” He adds that “nothing was beautiful and nothing was ugly.” He casts a cold eye on Penny’s mother, Gina, mocking her working-class accent and writing that she had “so much to express” on Facebook, “but not necessarily the skill or will to express those things in any personal, authentic, or original way.” The book reaches a crescendo with Penny’s trial. Defense lawyers portrayed a city lost to dysfunction; the police were too late to show up, and only Penny was left to put his life on the line to save “perfect strangers.” The prosecutor—who several times during the trial referred to the former Marine as “the white man” and “the white defendant”—acknowledged what activists and liberal politicians could not: Penny had “started out trying to do the right thing,” until he kept his choke hold in place too long and Neely died. A majority of the jurors were white, but at least four were not. They took 24 hours—not very long, given the case’s complexity—to acquit Penny of criminally negligent homicide. Afterward, Lander warned that the verdict would “valorize vigilantism.” The left-wing Working Families Party called it a “modern-day lynching.” But all of the jurors rode the subway at least semi-regularly; several had spoken before the trial of their own upsetting experiences on the train, and in the end they appeared disinclined to punish a man who acted to forestall what might have been imminent violence. It is a virtue of this book that Giridharadas, even while seeming to harbor sympathies for the prosecutors, applies an empathy for New Yorkers who try to thread their way through the divides created by this case. “They shared the sense of being undefended. They shared the sense that the calvary was not coming,” he writes. “They shared a sense that the system was rigged,” and so “shared the sense of their own powerlessness.” Just hours after the verdict, Penny walked along 30th Street, and people recognized him. “Almost to a person,” Giridharadas notes, “they shook his hand and thanked him.” It’s tempting to think of this, three years after the incident, as a snapshot of a more turbulent city. But as I finished this essay, I learned of a new horror: Early this month, Erin Piacenti was walking through Times Square, having freshly returned from maternity leave, when Pamela Cisneros, a middle-aged mother of three who appeared lost to madness, fatally stabbed Piacenti in the abdomen. Cisneros, who had suffered decades of mental-health issues, then tried to stab police officers; they shot and killed her. And for Piacenti, the young, innocent woman who lost her life, no one was there to save her. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?
2026-09-21

What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When I was 20 years old, I started to sense a hole inside me. The feeling was utterly concrete, though the result of no medical condition that any of the various doctors I saw could identify. The hole felt like a viscous emptiness—a paradox, admittedly, as any real emptiness should have felt like nothing. This was a nothing, but its nothingness very much felt like something—and something that was growing. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. Accompanying this feeling of an expanding hole at the center of me was a sense that I was losing more and more of myself to it, like shoreline eroded by rising waters. To lose myself felt less like oblivion than instability. I observed in myself a new susceptibility to the opinions of others, and a new recklessness, as if some dark, decisive action might either quiet my doubts about my fundamental worth or end my longing once and for all. I recall one particularly dangerous, drunken night that had me well over the legal limit, both aware that I was getting into a car that could kill me and others, and also not believing that any of it mattered. The onset of my devolving well-being was strange and sudden, and seemed connected to childhood in some way I couldn’t understand. I hadn’t been a tortured child but rather placid, if introverted. My parents always said that I had been easy to keep happy. It sometimes seemed to me as if the hole I was experiencing was one where some piece of my childhood had once resided. My friends found my behavior disturbing. I was missing class in college, showing up to theater rehearsals drunk. A caring young woman I was dating could take no more of my savage, ranting mood swings and persistent despair. She broke up with me over tea at the student union and implored me to seek help. I knew she was right. Even sleep was now fraught with violent, terrifying dreams—of rocket ships out of control, conflagrations and invasions, and, yes, holes into which I would disappear. But exactly what sort of help I might need, I didn’t know. I confided my woes to a friend who’d grown up on a farm in Kentucky. He never missed Sunday Mass, and when I told him about the hole inside me, he pulled a copy of The Seven Storey Mountain from his bag and handed it to me. Thomas Merton, he said, had written about something similar. It was what had led him to leave the world and become a monk. I found deep resonance in the book’s harrowing account of Merton’s time at Cambridge and Columbia—the intense restlessness and emptiness, the disgust and torpor and despair. But Merton’s solution was theistic: a turn to God that I could only read as naive and distorting. I didn’t have any interest in God. Even the passages that approached the specificity of my own experience were charged with personification that read as anachronistic at best: The room was very quiet. It was rather dark, too. And as I lay in bed, in my weariness and pain and disgust, I felt for a moment the shadow of another visitor pass into the room. It was death, that came to stand by my bed. I kept my eyes closed, more out of apathy than anything else. But anyway, there was no need to open one’s eyes to see the visitor, to see death. Death is someone you see very clearly with eyes in the center of your heart: eyes that see not by reacting to light, but by reacting to a kind of a chill from within the marrow of your own life. I knew this experience: a central chill, a lightlessness connected to the doomed feeling of some ultimate end. But I resisted any characterization that departed from the fundamental sensation itself—of a growing hole inside me. For no reason I knew, I was convinced that remaining honest with myself about this basic fact was the only thing I could do to help myself. My parents were worried. They flew out to visit me at school and figure out what was wrong. Over a meal they prepared for me in a hotel room with a kitchen, I confided in them. There was a hole inside me that I needed to address, I said. They were understandably perplexed. My mother found me a psychiatrist, an avuncular Brit who was more interested in my sex life than my interior void, which I insisted was more real than he seemed willing to allow. But something important endured from the handful of sessions I had with him. A volume on the shelf over his left ear drew my fascination, and would soon fill weeks of my life: Freud’s The Interpretation of Dreams. That book began a process in me, what I’ve often called “curiosity” when I’ve told this story, but which is probably better described as a form of relationship. The nightly tumult of disturbing dreams persisted, and even if I couldn’t be sure whether Freud was right about how to make sense of them, simply attempting to do so seemed to help. Paying attention made it easier to remember my dreams, and to remember more of them. In one, I was carefully drawing an empty circle on a page of the Quran that my grandparents had given me as a boy, a circle through which—impossibly—I could reach and touch cool water. I awoke with the feeling that the hole inside me might be leading to something better, however unknown. Even so, it wasn’t until I mentioned that I was interested in dreams to a teacher I admired that I started to keep a detailed nightly log. This teacher had done a great deal of dream-work, and suggested that I tie a pencil to my finger to remind me, in those brief moments of stirring between sleep cycles, to write down whatever fragments were stirring in hypnagogic consciousness. So I did. What resulted was a daily haul of interior riches. The vividness of my unadulterated hopes and sorrows, the unusual expansiveness and wonder I experienced by night, lived on in the often-paradoxical images I would recall throughout the day. My study of Freud led to a similar scouring of the work of other psychoanalysts—and, later, mystics such as Emanuel Swedenborg, Ibn Arabi, and Rabbi Nachman—for clues about what kind of sense to be making of my dreams. In my final year of college, I encountered, in a late work of Carl Jung’s, the advice to treat dreams as meaningful psychic events, a technique to facilitate what Jung called their “compensatory” function—balancing the biases and limitations of one’s conscious attitudes. I took this to mean treating a dream’s images as real in their own right. Whether doing so had any such balancing effect on me, I can’t be sure. But my persistent despair seemed to be dimming; the hole inside me felt different the more I was able to gaze patiently into it for whatever was coming through. The dream-work was paying dividends, though what exactly was paying them out, I had no clue. I know how what I’m about to share is likely to come off; it isn’t the first time I’ve spoken it aloud. I started to dream of things that hadn’t happened yet, to “see” things at night that then appeared in my life during the day. One of the earliest and most startling of these uncanny doublings took place in my first year after college. I was living and working in a small town in the Tuscan countryside, without a phone, before the internet. I dreamed that a friend I knew in Paris showed up on my doorstep with skis on his back. Two days later, I awoke to the sound of my name being called from down in the piazza, outside my front window. I opened the worn balcony doors to find the very friend I’d dreamed of; he’d gotten my Italian address from a mutual friend and seen that the town I was living in wasn’t too far out of the way as he made the trip from Rome up to the city in the Alps where he planned to spend a few days skiing. I resisted speaking of what had happened, lest I be impelled to name whatever in me had seized my bewildered attention. The Tuscan episode was not the last time dream and day seemed to cross wires. Years later, I would dream of a terrorist attack on Manhattan two days before 9/11, and of a malign, cough-inducing fog slowly engulfing the world several weeks before the eruption of the coronavirus into my (and the world’s) awareness—improbable coincidences I struggled to accept as such. And yet, these visions became the occasion to doubt any picture of the universe that failed to make room for what could only be experienced as a kind of magic. If the prognosticating mode had been the only benefit to accrue from my attention to my dreams, I might not have been so diligent in my recordkeeping. After all, I wasn’t seeing stock prices or lottery numbers, the only rationale many might have accepted for my habit of destroying every good night’s rest. For at that point, I was waking up every 45 minutes to reach for the bedside pad on which I made notes about whatever was moving around inside me. But my night work was opening up creative vistas. One mentor, well-versed in working with the unconscious, suggested that it was enlarging the space of what she kept calling—despite my confusion about what she really meant by it—my psyche. Indeed, a growing spaciousness was now my sense of the interior core where the hole had opened up. The hole itself wasn’t gone, but it had changed character, less a wound now than an opening, though I couldn’t always tell the difference. And yet, the trajectory seemed to be shifting. That pliant, receptive brightness bearing the imprint of something more than just my name, the self, began to feel heightened, enlarged. I had learned that some cultures conceived of the self as containing two parts: a smaller, individual, daily self, and a larger, multiple, primordial one. This greater self, it was believed, was the one that dreamed. Something about this way of thinking felt more useful—and more reflective of my experience—than any of the latest studies in neuroscience I read about the physiological functions of dreams. Psyche : Greek for “soul.” Which felt like a more appropriate frame of reference—despite my continued resistance to the language of theism—for experiences that so evidently defied the materialist doctrines of my late-20th-century education. To privilege the Newtonian model of classical mechanics—one object pushing or pulling another, all the way down to the molecules that formed consciousness—over the actual data of my experience felt like intellectual laziness. I understood that others would see my willingness to abide more patiently in the mystery as irrational. My usage of the Greek term started to give way to its English corollary. The word soul seemed to make better sense of whatever was expanding inside me, a rising that was also a deepening, married to the uncanny knowing of what seemed like prognostication, and, more valuably, to a natural empathy for others. And in case this comes off as more altruistic than seems reasonable: Less blinded by myself, more porous to others, I found, too, that I was becoming a better writer. From the November 2024 issue: Jeffrey Goldberg, Ayad Akhtar, Robert Downey Jr., and Bartlett Sher on writing in the age of AI Soul was, for me then, a mode of being mostly experienced in images by night that shaped my understanding by day—for example, that a close friend was like the abandoned dog I dreamed he was, long neglected by his family, never allowed into the house; an image that helped me navigate his touchiness, providing readier access to the wellspring of loving loyalty that coursed beneath his habitual choler. I found in the use of soul a capaciousness that not only accounted for this richer relationship to others and the world—and to myself—but served as a bridge to deeper mystery, and to a new patience that allowed me to acclimate to it. There were other benefits, too. Just as the vital, vivid stuff of my brain’s nightlife enriched my being—to use a word that joined soul in the lexicon of descriptive terms of dubious provenance—so did my immersion in dream dross seem to feed my capacities with language. There was a stronger tether now between word and thing, a tether, paradoxically, that operated simultaneously in many directions and layers. The webbed, protean mode of associative thinking—one thing reaching for another, in opposing vectors at once—was less an effort than a reflex, more instinctive than the ordinal fixity of my usual habits of mind. Shakespeare, whom I’d long struggled to apprehend, now made easier sense, even as the experience of reading him felt like perpetual entry into undiscovered country, the phonetic expatiations, the dense semantic clusters, the sounds and images opening up more and more effortlessly into a particular state of absorptive flow, a pleasing emotional and intellectual static that I took to calling “linguistic space.” I started taking a yoga class on the Upper West Side. Our becalming teacher spent the better part of our weekly hour teaching us to meditate. Setting aside my irritation with her hushed and holy tones, I found myself growing more susceptible to her instructions. I could catch a gap between thoughts that felt curiously similar to a rich blankness that resulted from hours spent in linguistic space. The fixed point of focus on the breath sharpened my mind and filled it with something closer to light than thought. On my own, I began to sit—sometimes for hours—in this pregnant blankness, exhibiting a facility for what the yoga teacher called “practice” that I couldn’t explain. My weekly hour with that yoga teacher sparked an interest in meditation that led to weeks, and years, of working with other instructors of inner techniques. I learned to visualize and hold in memory shapes derived from Gnostic practices and colors derived from Sufi ones. Intoning Abulafia’s list of Hebrew names for God at the proper pitch and volume shook the bones in my ear and nerves along my neck. Calm was promised, and often followed, though I wondered whether this said more about my susceptibility to suggestion than the practices themselves. Sometimes what followed was stranger. Once, as I sat in a darkened room at dusk, observing the breath going in and out of me, I felt that breath begin to breathe itself. In the half shadows, with my eyes closed, a startling brightness waxed and waned with the movement of air. What was this light? I wondered. Was this just the room’s faint light filtering through my eyelids, brightening and dimming as my pupils dilated and contracted with each breath? If so, was this suggestion too? I would come to no definitive answer. The inner light continued to strobe. If it was all suggestion, I didn’t mind. It was getting me further than faith had. From Meister Eckhart I learned to recognize in my meditative blankness a silence clean of longing, one that, if I could remain in it, would release an astonishing vitality. Others I studied with found it strange that my interest in meditation would be piqued by dream-work and quickened by pentameter. They sometimes remarked that what I was up to resembled anthropology more than devotion. They were only partly right. To me, it was all a study in being, or soul, or spirit (the latest addition to my lexicon)—which is to say, an inquiry into the substance and nature of the interior life. My reading, too, reflected the scavenging of a magpie in search of material from which to build its nest: the Phaedrus and Patanjali and Mulla Sadra; Being and Time one month, Mathnawi the next. What exactly I thought I was doing was not a matter of concern as long as I kept to myself about it. What I’m compressing into paragraphs here took so much longer, of course, and was hardly as sequential as telling makes it sound. I kept at it for the reward of the work itself. I had experiences of rapture (and sorrow) so deep, of pleasures so unprecedented, it was sometimes hard to return to the dishes and the rent. But I had studied with a remarkable Vedic teacher who warned me about seeking states rather than the truth. “What is the truth?” I once asked him as we sauntered along a Tennessee creek. He pulled his orange robe tighter against a rising spring wind. He was too smart to oblige. “The truth is not like knowledge about that tree,” he said, pointing to a blooming magnolia. “Only the tree knows its truth.” I didn’t want to pretend I understood him. “You write stories,” he said. “Inside the story is a meaning that is the reason for it. Not words that carry it, but the wordless substance of its truth you take away.” Despite my customary reticence about such things, I had occasions to compare notes with fellow travelers. One evening, at a fundraising function downtown, I found myself sharing martinis with a physician whose lived-in eloquence about his Zen practice felt like an opening. I was not expecting the lesson in brain science he proceeded to impart: I’d become addicted, he said, to activating certain parts of my cerebral cortex. Like an addict—I flinched at that word—I was pulling on neurological switches, training my brain to release its own pleasure chemicals through a growing grab bag of inner tricks. Clearly he didn’t approve. I was too embarrassed to ask why, even as I recalled my teacher’s warning not to chase states, but rather to seek the wordless substance of truth. I resisted the physician’s account even as I couldn’t ignore the unease it stirred in me. My fellow traveler was right, at least as far as neuroscience went. Studies were beginning to show that meditation—a loose term for the various practices of inner focus I was involved with—activated and deactivated specific parts of the human brain. In the 1990s, the neuroscientist Andrew Newberg studied advanced Tibetan monks and Franciscan nuns as they entered deep meditative states. The results were strikingly similar. In both groups, areas of the brain believed to be responsible for orienting us in space and time showed markedly decreased activity, as if the neural basis for the boundary between self and other had gone quiet. They were using very different techniques, but the results, neurologically speaking, were nearly indistinguishable. Each group, however, described the experience differently: the nuns as a feeling of union with Christ, the monks as a state of emptiness. I knew enough about Buddhism to recognize in that word, emptiness, something very different from the hole that had opened up inside me during early adulthood. For the Buddhist, perception of the nature of reality discloses the identity of emptiness with form, and form with emptiness. It’s a perception described in other traditions by other means, and sometimes, in Christianity, as an annihilation of the individual personality in the presence of God. The maps and words and techniques were different; somehow, the underlying experience itself, beyond words or ideas, was the same. Not quite in the words of my Vedic teacher: The brain scans were a story containing a truth known only to the one inside them. For a long time, all of this felt like little more than a motley, life-enhancing assortment, which is not to downplay the valuable place it had in my life. Rightly or wrongly, I attributed the return of my fundamental stability after the harrowing years of early adulthood—and the return of the placid, placable self I’d known much earlier in life—to my commitment to daily practice. I would come to think of inner work as an adult corollary to the joyous absorption of childhood play. I’d found a way to play again, I concluded, and somehow this had seemed to “fill” the hole again. But the life of the spirit was not what I took my real life to be. I was a writer. I wanted to know the broken world that was, not the healed one that could be. I had no fundamental interest in escaping the human condition. To the extent that spirit—or soulfulness—anchored my curiosity, quickened my perceptions, deepened my empathy, I rationalized its creative and analytical utility. In truth, something less tangible than optimization bound me to practice. It kept me in the company of more eternal conundrums, questions of consciousness and origin and meaning for which, of course, I expected no answer. But proximity to the unknown—living contact with it, if you will—seemed like a worthy form of knowledge. In this, I was like many friends who took psychedelics; I wanted to recover the awe and mystery I recognized in their accounts when they shared them. For the most part, though, I was still doing what my teacher had warned me against—chasing states. In part, this was a function of resisting meaning. I had been too respectful of my teacher back then to tell him I wasn’t sure that a story’s meaning mattered to me the way he assumed it would. But I felt the slow accretion of a wordless substance inside me, not well-being or certainty or the rapture of some fugitive sublime, but something I might call intimacy: intimacy with an inwardness spied only indirectly, whose purer acquaintance I seemed to be making very slowly indeed. With the hindsight of decades, I can track a tectonic shift away from the visible to something more diffuse and sustaining, an interior through line that has both warned me against the tyranny of acquisition—of things, of states—and provided the means of finding sustenance in absence. Whatever this is, whatever name I might give it, I can’t deny that it all seems to have proceeded from that perception of a hole I felt or imagined when I was younger. William James might have called it a “sick soul”; I still don’t feel entirely confident about what word to use for the continuity that I’ve found or constructed, though some word has always been necessary. I had spent years resisting every vocabulary that exceeded the raw data of sensation, only to find that this austerity, too, had to be exceeded. Too much of what mattered most in my own life had remained unspoken not because it was unreal, but because I did not know how to bring it into language without betraying it. For decades, I steered conversations away from these inner experiences, obedient to the rule that such things could not be talked about without sounding foolish or deranged. But my reticence began to feel like capitulation to falseness. It took me years to understand that my silence was not only protective but deforming—for without others to hear it, the still, small voice within was so much harder to discern. And yet, the silence was not my own to break. With so little room in our common life for such things, we have no common language for them either. Ralph Waldo Emerson knew the problem. In “The Over-Soul,” he wrote that language could not paint this “subtile” order of our experience in its own colors; but he knew how important it was to keep trying. The great Russian novelists knew it too, treating soul not as a word reserved for Sundays, but as the moral substance of life. I’ve found myself mourning not their metaphysics but their seriousness, their acceptance of the inscrutable boundlessness of the inner life—unruly, morally consequent—as a subject for public speech. What matters to me now is less the defense of any particular term than the search for a language in which such things can be spoken of seriously, in fiction and elsewhere, without piety or diagnosis. Without a language for speaking openly together about the inner life, what often matters most to us remains locked away, inaccessible to the inquiry and challenge vital to all knowledge. As I finished a draft of this essay, I had a dream. In it, I stood at the edge of a well—another hole, I realized as I made note the following morning—where there was no bucket to draw water. Around it moved countless people in a flurried hubbub, shepherds and masons and architects, all of us trying to figure out how to draw the water whose surface we could see in the faint shimmering at the bottom. It was going to take time, I thought, before we figured it out—just as I awoke. This article appears in the November 2026 print edition with the headline “In My Dreams.” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

In Defense of Guilt by Association
2026-09-21

In Defense of Guilt by Association

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
J. D. Vance and Abdul El-Sayed have very little in common ideologically and seem to like each other even less. As part of a nasty exchange of personal insults last month, the Democratic nominee for Senate in Michigan implied that Vance’s grandfather would disapprove of the vice president’s Indian American wife. In response, Vance called El-Sayed “very, very evil” and warned him to “keep my wife’s name the hell out of your mouth because she’s way out of your league.” Yet even as Vance and El-Sayed are happy to trade schoolyard taunts, they can agree on at least one thing: that guilt by association is a contemptible means of scoring cheap political points. “I’m tired of this guilt-by-association thing,” El-Sayed complained earlier this year, when he was asked about his decision to campaign alongside Hasan Piker, an internet shock jock who has said that “America deserved 9/11,” described allegations of sexual violence on October 7 as “rape fantasies,” and praised Mao Zedong as “one of the great leaders of the world,” among many other charming bon mots. Despite repeated calls from within his own party to renounce Piker, El-Sayed refuses to do so. Vance is facing a similar situation with his friend Tucker Carlson. Vance “doesn’t believe in guilt-by-association cancel culture,” a spokesperson said in September 2024, after the then-senator appeared onstage with the conservative podcast host. In the years since his firing from Fox News, Carlson has lauded a Holocaust denier as “the most important historian in the United States,” accused a small Jewish sect of orchestrating the war in Iran, and praised Vladimir Putin as “the most effective leader in my lifetime.” Carlson, whose show returns again and again to the supposed perfidy of the Jews, has become the country’s highest-profile promoter of anti-Semitism. He recently held a friendly conversation with a guest who claimed that algebra was “fake math” created for “goy slaves.” Vance was asked directly about his relationship with Carlson at a White House press conference earlier this month. “Tucker is a friend, despite the fact that we disagree on a number of things,” the vice president said. “And I’m not going to play the game where I throw friends under the bus because I have political disagreements with them. I think that is a terrible place for the United States to go.” Jonathan Chait: J. D. Vance’s disingenuous defense of Tucker Carlson El-Sayed and Vance would have us believe that their troublesome friends are no different from the people in our own lives whose political views we abhor but whom we nonetheless love and appreciate. “If I had to choose between betraying my country and betraying my friend, I hope I should have the guts to betray my country,” the English writer E. M. Forster wrote in 1938 as Europe hurtled toward war. It’s a noble sentiment, a paean to friendship over tribe. But Piker and Carlson are not the equivalent of your crazy uncle. And El-Sayed and Vance are not regular citizens; they’re political figures whose judgment and choices affect other Americans’ lives. One’s choice of friends is in fact an excellent sign of character, and in this respect, both Vance and El-Sayed have demonstrated a conspicuous lack of it. The tactic known as “guilt by association” evokes the legacy of Senator Joe McCarthy, whose feral zeal for exposing Communists, real or imagined, was so era-defining that it inspired the attachment of an ism to his surname. McCarthy’s sin wasn’t his anti-communism, however—there were indeed highly placed Soviet agents in the federal government. It was his recklessness and lack of concern for the truth. There has to be a reasonable middle ground between conducting a witch hunt and blatantly ignoring someone’s willingness to fraternize with genuinely disreputable people. Now that their associations are in the spotlight, El-Sayed, Vance, and their respective ideological compatriots are eager to minimize them. In a softball interview with Piker several months ago, the Pod Save America host Jon Favreau, a prominent liberal opinion-shaper, said that he wants Democrats to take the “Don’t Talk About Hasan Piker For A Day Challenge.” Asked about Piker’s remarks concerning 9/11, El-Sayed tried to depict himself as a victim of the “cancel culture that all of us agreed was a bad idea back in 2020, but we seem to want to bring back.” A “Vance ally” echoed a similar sentiment to The Washington Post about the vice president’s relationship with Carlson, saying, “We’re done with the canceling, period.” No one should accept these evasions. In April, El-Sayed deployed Piker to boost support among young voters at Michigan State and the University of Michigan. In the final hours before polls closed, El-Sayed appeared at a pool party with Piker and other internet influencers in Detroit. Vance’s association with Carlson goes much deeper. In a 2021 interview recalling the time they first met, Vance described their immediate recognition of each other as “the guy who’s exactly right about what’s going on in the country.” Carlson helped persuade Donald Trump to select Vance as his running mate; Vance hired Carlson’s son as his deputy press secretary. When Vance guest-hosted Charlie Kirk’s show after the conservative activist’s assassination, one of the first people he invited into the vice-presidential suite was Carlson. (At Kirk’s funeral six days later, Carlson delivered a eulogy that seemed to imply that the Jews had murdered Kirk just as they had Jesus Christ.) The most obvious reason El-Sayed and Vance haven’t disowned their noxious friends is that both Piker and Carlson command large audiences. Politics is about winning votes, and a moral miscreant’s is worth the same as a saint’s. Other politicians have done much worse than associate with the likes of Piker and Carlson. What’s more troubling is that El-Sayed and Vance both appear to sympathize with at least some of what their influencer friend espouses. On multiple occasions, Vance has shown that he either doesn’t understand or doesn’t care about anti-Semitism. He once dismissed outrage over a group of Young Republicans joking about gas chambers as “pearl clutching” and characterized attempts to sideline bigots from the conservative movement as “endless, self-defeating purity tests.” One might think that Carlson’s frequent attacks on Vance’s boss, up to and including that Trump “should be removed from office immediately,” would have persuaded Vance, purely out of his own self-interest, to disavow Carlson. That he hasn’t done so indicates the high value he places on his association with him. If not for such data points, Vance’s friendship with Carlson would be easy to dismiss as misplaced affection for someone who helped advance his career. But Vance is on the record stating otherwise. Late last year, he said, I find the idea that Tucker Carlson—who has one of the largest podcasts in the world, who has millions of listeners, who supported Donald Trump in the 2024 election, who supported me in the 2024 election—the idea that his views are somehow completely anathema to conservatism, that he has no place in the conservative movement, is frankly absurd. This implies that Holocaust denial, claims of Jewish disloyalty to the United States, veneration of Putin, and a host of other poisonous ideas are welcome in the conservative movement. Even the white-nationalist influencer Nick Fuentes is fed up with Vance’s disingenuousness. “JD Vance panders to the far right but simply doesn’t want the baggage of associating with the far right,” Fuentes told The New York Times. “If he has a problem with ‘antisemites,’ then he should start by disavowing his best friend Tucker Carlson.” A similar ideological affinity appears to exist between Piker and El-Sayed, whose past statements on 9/11 and terrorism more broadly have a way of creating a moral equivalency between the United States and its Islamist enemies. “Today, I mourn the 3K lives, 6K injuries, & infrastructural devastation in NYC, perpetuated ignorantly in the name of my faith,” El-Sayed tweeted five years ago on the 20th anniversary of the attacks. “Tomorrow, I’ll mourn ~1M lives, millions of injuries, & infrastructural devastation in 3 countries, perpetuated ignorantly in the name of my country.” Earlier this year, after a Hezbollah-inspired terrorist rammed his car into a Detroit-area synagogue where 140 preschool-age Jewish children were playing, El-Sayed said that “hurt people hurt people,” a reference to the fact that the attacker had lost four relatives in an Israeli air strike. (In a clumsy attempt at an apology, El-Sayed stubbornly refused to acknowledge the odious logic of his remarks, allowing only that he had chosen to administer “the right medicine at the wrong time.”) Like Prince Hal banishing Falstaff from the royal court, sometimes ending a friendship is a sign of the maturity necessary to lead. Barack Obama made just such a choice in 2008 after the hateful and conspiratorial remarks of his longtime pastor, Jeremiah Wright, were exposed to the world. Obama’s relationship with Wright was much more intimate than that between El-Sayed and Piker or Vance and Carlson; he had sat in Wright’s pews for decades, and Wright had baptized his children. And yet Obama ultimately turned his back on Wright, saying that the preacher’s divisive, anti-American “rants” were “an insult to what we’ve been trying to do in this campaign.” Obama understood that as long as he was associated with a character like Wright, he could never be a credible advocate for the national unity he claimed to seek. J. D. Vance and Abdul El-Sayed each face a much easier decision—and each has failed to make the right one. It’s a testament to Americans’ increasing tolerance for extremism that both men seem to be getting away with it.

The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex
2026-09-21

The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When Christina Kattula, a 31-year-old yoga teacher in Michigan, was ready to start a family in 2024, she hoped for a son. Her husband “wanted a boy to bond with,” she told me. “And if we had girls later, he thought it would be cute to have a protective-big-brother-type thing.” Kattula decided to try something she’d heard about on a podcast: the so-called Babydust Method. The theory, which was first laid out in a self-published book from 2016, claims, contrary to scientific evidence, that couples can influence their baby’s sex by precisely timing when they have intercourse. Why not try it? she thought. What’s the harm if it doesn’t work? Kattula and her husband followed the book’s advice, and she became pregnant with a boy. During her pregnancy, Kattula posted a TikTok to her few hundred followers recommending the book to other couples. “Blessed either way,” she said in the video. “But it worked for us. If you’re on your last kid and you’re really, really trying for something, read The Babydust Method.” She’s one of many women on TikTok and elsewhere on social media who credit Babydust with helping them conceive a child of their preferred sex. Some acknowledge that the method is scientifically unfounded, while other posters are more credulous: In one video, Julie Wise, a mom of two in Massachusetts, said that it’s “not like a fact; I’m pretty sure it’s just like a theory,” but that she is nevertheless “kind of a believer.” In Wisconsin, a day-care owner and mom of four named Courtney Raspor said the method has something like a “90 percent success rate.” Some go a step beyond what the book recommends, into the realm of the woo: In Australia, Faye Briotti said that in addition to timing when she had sex, she also kept pink crystals and salt by her bed, and painted her nails pink. A woman named Reagan, who’d had two boys and was hoping for a girl, said that she got rid of all her baby boy clothes, “because I really wanted to put out into the universe that I wanted a girl.” Read: The best parenting hack is being rich The rise of TikTok and other social-media platforms has given audiences ready access to folk-science life hacks of all kinds. People can now more quickly find vertical videos about scientifically suspect practices such as mouth-taping and cortisol-lowering than they can make a doctor’s appointment to address snoring or weight gain. Conceiving and carrying a pregnancy—high-stakes experiences that are full of uncertainty—seem to make some women unusually susceptible to dubious strategies. Kathryn Taylor, the author of The Babydust Method, got the idea when she was preparing to conceive her first child. She wanted to see if she could increase her odds of having a boy—she thought her husband would be more excited about the baby if it was. She is not a medical doctor and doesn’t have a Ph.D.; she has a bachelor’s degree in microbiology, immunology, and molecular genetics, and now works in pharmaceutical sales. But she knew that an embryo’s sex is determined by the sperm that takes part in creating it: If the sperm carries a Y chromosome, the embryo will be a boy; if it has an X chromosome, the embryo will be a girl. And so she wondered if she could encourage a Y-bearing sperm to fertilize her egg. She read studies about reproduction on PubMed, a medical database, and came away believing that, after a man and woman have sex, Y sperm undergo an activation process called capacitation faster than X sperm do, but that they also deteriorate sooner. Therefore, she posited, having sex as close as possible to the day the woman ovulates should give boy sperm the best chance of getting to the egg first. By the same logic, having sex a few days before ovulation would allow the boy sperm time to die off before the egg arrives, and increase the likelihood that an X sperm will fertilize the egg. Sandra Carson, an ob-gyn and reproductive endocrinologist at Yale who has written about sex-selection efforts since the 1980s, and Michael Zinaman, a reproductive endocrinologist in New York, told me that X and Y sperm don’t move or activate at different rates, that one doesn’t live longer than the other, and that the timing or frequency of intercourse does not determine the fetus’s sex. Taylor told me she was “100 percent” sure the method was going to work for her. As she writes in her author bio, she had “a boy first and a girl second, just like she planned.” She decided to write the book, she told me, after a few moms who had “three boys or whatever” asked her how she had managed to get one child of each sex, and after she shared her tips, she said, they were successful too. She set out to create a short, readable guide—the Kindle version of Babydust is 82 pages long—to help other women. Taylor has claimed that the method works about 87 percent of the time; more than 50,000 people now belong to a Babydust Method Facebook group, which requires proof of book purchase—$10 on Amazon—to join. From the September 2026 issue: The great romance slump When I asked Taylor about Carson and Zinaman’s research, she stuck by her theory. She told me that doctors might not understand the method because they work with patients rather than researching sperm. (Carson has researched sperm extensively.) Taylor emphasized that she thinks a crucial difference between X and Y sperm is their capacitation, and even if that’s proved wrong, she said, it “doesn’t debunk the theory.” It would just mean, she believes, that male and female sperm are distinct in a way “that’s microscopic, that’s genetic, and it’s deep inside the cell.” Attempts to naturally influence a baby’s sex have a long history. Hippocrates believed that having intercourse immediately after a woman’s period would increase the odds of conceiving a son; doing so “a good while” later, he thought, would favor a girl. In the 1960s, an ob-gyn named Landrum Shettles hypothesized that Y sperm are faster but more fragile, and that to conceive a boy, a couple should have sex as close to ovulation as possible. The following decade, a public-health researcher named Elizabeth Whelan proposed entirely different advice: that couples seeking to conceive a boy should have sex four to six days before ovulation. Babydust is the updated, Millennial-pink gloss on some of these old ideas. In her book, Taylor argues that although the essence of the Shettles method is correct, the science behind it was wrong. The reality is that none of these ideas are proved to work, and neither is Babydust. At least, they don’t have a better success rate than the 50 or so percent chance a couple has of conceiving either a boy or girl anyway. Zinaman said that if techniques such as Babydust were sound, ob-gyns and fertility doctors would tell patients about them. But, he said, “we don’t have anything we can use to assist patients that way.” The only process that can guarantee having a boy or a girl is in vitro fertilization, which allows parents-to-be to choose the sex of the embryo that gets transferred into the mother. (American doctors are allowed, but not encouraged, to offer IVF for non-medically-necessary sex selection.) This is now such settled science that the most recent definitive study on the topic is from 1995, when researchers recruited 221 women who wanted to become pregnant, tracked their hormones and dates of intercourse, and found that “the timing of sexual intercourse in relation to ovulation has no influence on the sex of the baby.” The author of that study, Allen Wilcox, who once led the Reproductive Epidemiology Group at the National Institutes of Health, told me that no further studies have been conducted about these methods because “you can’t get a paper published saying there’s no association, because every scientist knows that.” Read: The problem with child-led potty training One reason some people might believe that Babydust is worth trying, despite the lack of evidence, is that it offers a sense of control. All the stages of bringing a baby into the world—trying to conceive, being pregnant, delivering the child, caring for a newborn—can inspire magical thinking in some parents, precisely because so much is out of their hands. One study of hundreds of women undergoing fertility treatments found that 40 percent of them performed a good-luck ritual on the day of their procedure; another, of pregnant Jewish women in Israel, found that they scrupulously avoided buying baby furniture before the baby’s birth in order to avoid “tempting fate.” When my son was still breech at 37 weeks of pregnancy—when most babies have already flipped into the head-down birthing position—my ob-gyn recommended a website of the most batty methods imaginable to get him to rotate. This is how I, a skeptical science writer, found myself standing on my head in a swimming pool one morning and having a “heart to heart” with my fetus about the importance of turning. (This did not work.) It doesn’t help that the first question people typically ask a mother-to-be—Are you having a boy or girl?—is something she has no power over. Many Babydust adherents with whom I spoke and whose videos I watched didn’t seem to believe in Babydust as much as they wanted to believe in something. “People are hoping to alter fate,” Zinaman said, “and half the time it alters fate and they’re happy, and the other half, Oh well, it was never 100 percent anyway.” It can’t hurt to try was something I heard often. Raspor, the Wisconsin day-care owner who used Babydust to conceive a girl, told me, “There’s no way to know whether it was the method or simply chance,” but, “I personally don’t see the harm in giving it a try, as long as you understand that there are no guarantees.” At other points in history, and in certain cultures, many parents hoped for a boy rather than a girl. Today, though, that broad preference seems to have shifted. The Economist reported last year that American and some European couples who have a girl first are more likely to stop at one child than couples who have a boy first, and that adoptive parents are willing to pay a premium for a girl. Taylor told me that, according to nonrepresentative surveys she’s conducted, two-thirds of her method’s practitioners are hoping to conceive a girl. Thus, another factor likely playing a “huge role” in this trend is sex preference, Brittany Stahnke Joy, a social-work professor at East Tennessee State University, told me. Although precise numbers aren’t available, Stahnke Joy estimates that 30 to 40 percent of expectant parents at some point experience a strong desire for a certain gender and some sadness if they find out they’re having the other. The women I spoke with seemed to sense that they’re not supposed to have sex preferences. Wise, the mom and content creator in Massachusetts, used Babydust when conceiving her daughter three years ago, after learning about it on a pregnancy-app discussion board. “Obviously, a healthy baby is the main goal,” she told me, “but we can’t deny that we do have preferences sometimes.” Kattula, the Michigan yoga teacher, expressed misgivings about the concept of trying to influence a child’s sex, even though she was pleased to be having a boy. “Is it effed-up to pick and choose what you think the gender of your kids should be?” she said. “I don’t know.” A number of desires might motivate sex preferences and gender disappointment, Stahnke Joy said: With their child, a parent might hope to recreate themselves, or a relationship with their father or mother; or they might look forward to certain activities that they can imagine doing only with either a daughter or a son. Most of the time, she said, these feelings resolve once the baby is born, but “some people have a lot more children just to try to get the girl.” This is partly why, as much as I know Babydust doesn’t work, I also understand wanting to believe. When I was pregnant, I initially wanted a girl. I thought girls were smarter and more verbal, their games were more imaginative, and they were better behaved, even though intellectually I realized this was not always true. I wanted to play Fairy Princess Unicorns instead of Garbage Trucks. Two and a half years later, I do play an awful lot of Garbage Trucks. I sometimes get jealous when I see a toddler girl sitting patiently at storytime while my son, Evan, runs wildly around the room. But Evan has also upended some of my expectations about gender: He is very talkative, has a great sense of humor, and is extremely smart. He loves stories and singing, and even getting dressed up occasionally. I sometimes wonder if, had I gotten my wish and conceived a girl, my hypothetical daughter would have any of those traits or interests. I realize that I don’t care, because I love my son exactly the way he is. Maybe the true magic dust of parenthood is that your baby will surprise you, no matter what their sex turns out to be.

GNOME 2 was definitely better than the KDE of its day. You can run Cinnamon if you miss GNOME 2, but when I tried it, it just felt old :) I've used it pretty exclusively since the 1.x days, though I did make a bit of a detour to Gnome 2.x for awhile. It mostly excels at being a decently well-integrated desktop environment with all the bits working together in a fairly consistent way with similar interfaces and setups. If you're coming from Windows where (at least until recentlyish) it also does a decent job of having a lot of integrated, consistent tooling it can ease the transition plus it mostly just works fine. yes, gnome went crap, and took the toolkit with it however mate or xfce is much better than even cinnamon. Only problem I have is really being able to see if I like mate or xfce, but am leaning more to xfce maybe. If you use complex applications you already have most of a DE installed on your system. I started dabbling with Linux in 99 or 00' and back then I liked KDE as it felt like a bridge of sorts from Windows. Slackware had packages for KDE so I tried that distro. Back then I had all the time in the world to dig through config files and what not. Slackware was great for this as pretty much everything was configured via one file or another. You really learn a lot about how the OS works, down to the kernel level, when you were install Slackware at that time. Other distros such as Mandrake tried pushing everything through GUI tools and as often as not they wouldn't work and you would be stuck going to config files. [govinfo.gov] The hand-waving you open with doesn't apply to what you cited. You cited a statute that merely shifts it so that a class can sue before the anticipated harm, whereas normally you'd have to wait for the harm to happen to sue. "The product might have been better" isn't a harm. The issue is not that a single company's product will not improve as quickly. If all the big AI players sign on, then subscribers are stuck with a non-competitive market with lost value for their subscriptions. Frankly I see the AI companies prevailing, not because the plaintiffs can't show harm, but because they can claim a "public safety exception."
